# Political Theory — Topic 07: Nature of Politics and Communitarianism (Live Learning Session)

This is a complete teaching session, not a revision sheet. It is built to be read straight through by someone meeting the material for the first time, and it is built so that every claim can be traced to the page it came from.

This topic does something none of the previous six does. Topics 01 to 06 each examined a doctrine; this one examines the **subject matter itself** — what makes an activity political at all — and then shows that three rival political theories give three different answers because they begin from three different pictures of what society **is**. That is why the chapter ends with a table aligning the liberal, Marxist and communitarian views with **liberty, equality and fraternity**: the disagreement is not about policy but about social ontology.

The topic also carries a specific hazard. Communitarianism is the easiest position in the whole subject to caricature — as collectivism, as anti-rights, as a demand for enforced unanimity, or as a vague preference for "community values". The chapter itself, and the subject owners, block each of those readings explicitly, and this session reproduces the blocks rather than summarising them.

## How to read the evidence markers

| Marker | What it means |
|---|---|
| `> blockquote` | **Source wording reproduced** from the stated page, subject to the normalisations catalogued in the Source Ledger. Where the chapter is itself quoting someone — Ball, Easton, Roberts, Miliband, Engels, Marx — the lead-in says so and names the attribution the chapter gives. |
| **Bold** | Ordinary emphasis, key terms and editorial labels. ⚠️ Bold is **not** a quotation marker and carries no claim of word-for-word identity with the source. |
| ✅ | A claim the source states directly. |
| ⚠️ | Inference, caution, an unresolved tension, or a limit on what the evidence supports. |
| ❌ | A boundary — material this topic does not own, or a claim that must not be made. |
| ➡️ | A routing pointer to another topic's or subject's owner. |
| 🔶 | Content from the subject owner's extension sections that carries **no** anchor in the chapter's page range. Always separately marked. |
| 📰 | A dated anchor, carried with its date attached. |

⚠️ No exhaustive claim is made that every bolded phrase matches the source token for token. Where compression was necessary, the sentence is written as this session's own prose. Blockquotes and spans explicitly introduced as the chapter's wording are reproduced from the extraction, subject to the catalogued OCR and presentational corrections.

## Where the evidence comes from

- **Chapter of record:** O. P. Gauba, *An Introduction to Political Theory*, Fifth Edition **2009**, **Chapter 3, "Nature of Politics"** — **PDF pp.94-107 = printed pp.80-93**, an offset of **14**.
- **Canonical owners:** the subject's `basic/07` and `advanced/07` files, read in full. The basic owner's **§§13-16** carry doctrinal material **beyond** the chapter and are marked 🔶 throughout; its **§17** is the executable answer architecture, carried at Lesson 24.
- ⚠️ **Edition discipline.** The chapter's historical illustrations — the former **USSR (1917)** and the **People's Republic of China (1949)**, and its remarks on how their systems worked — are **2009** observations. The life-dates it prints for living thinkers are likewise frozen at 2009 and one of them is now closed; see the Source Ledger.

## The 24-lesson roadmap

| # | Lesson | The question it answers |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | Two senses of 'politics', and the layman's image | What is the word being used to mean? |
| 2 | From *polis* to social process | Why did politics stop being "the science of the state"? |
| 3 | The political situation | What must be present before an activity is political? |
| 4 | Ball's nursery example and its two defects | Which conflicts are **not** political? |
| 5 | Easton: the authoritative allocation of values | What makes a settlement binding? |
| 6 | Roberts's definition; politics against economics | What marks politics off from neighbouring processes? |
| 7 | Politicization, and its degrees | Who gets drawn into politics, and how far? |
| 8 | Characteristic 1 — conflict over scarce values | Why does scarcity matter? |
| 9 | Characteristic 2 — public goals and decisions | How does a private quarrel become political? |
| 10 | Characteristic 3 — authoritative decisions | Why do losers comply? |
| 11 | Characteristics 4 and 5 — interest groups, and the first fork | Where do liberal and Marxist accounts first diverge? |
| 12 | Politics as a dimension of the social process | What is politics a part of? |
| 13 | The three views introduced | What is the one question they answer differently? |
| 14 | Liberal view I — group activity and state activity | Who acts, and on whom? |
| 15 | Liberal view II — conciliation, common good, welfare state | Where does the liberal argument end up? |
| 16 | Marxist view I — primacy of economics; rational production | What has to be true for cooperation to be possible? |
| 17 | Marxist view II — Engels, irreconcilability, withering away | Why can politics not solve the conflict? |
| 18 | Communitarian view I — cooperation, not conflict | What is the essence of human nature on this view? |
| 19 | Communitarian view II — community, identity, two common goods | How do the two common goods differ? |
| 20 | Communitarian view III — MacIntyre, Taylor, Sandel | What exactly does each criticise? |
| 21 | Miliband: compromise against cooperation | Is managed conflict the same as harmony? |
| 22 | The scope-conditions verdict, and the summary table | Which view fits which society? |
| 23 | 🔶 Owner extensions — mechanisms, precursors, objection-reply chains, Indian application | What does an examiner expect beyond the chapter? |
| 24 | Separating the three views; answer architecture | How is this written under time pressure? |

*Caption: the chapter is a funnel followed by a fork. Steps 1 to 5 narrow "politics" to a precise object; step 6 shows that three theories describe that same object incompatibly; step 7 refuses to crown a winner.*

*Caption: the opening is a clearing operation. Note that Ball's two problems are **opposite** errors — one makes politics too narrow in **scope**, the other too narrow in **character** — and the chapter must defeat both.*

### 1. Two senses of the term

> The term 'politics' is applied to a particular social phenomenon as well as to a systematic study of that phenomenon. When Aristotle adopted Politics as the title of his famous work, he used the term to indicate a distinct branch of study.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.3, PDF p.94 (reproduced as the chapter's wording)

⚠️ **The ambiguity is not a nuisance to be tidied away; it is the chapter's first teaching point.** ⚠️ The same word names a **thing in the world** and the **study of that thing**, which is unusual — we do not use "biology" for living organisms. ⚠️ Keeping the two apart matters because a question asking about "the nature of politics" is asking about the **activity**, while a question about approaches or methods is asking about the **discipline**. ➡️ The discipline sense is the subject of the next chapter and its owner.

✅ **Five writers are named for the disciplinary sense**, each with a work: **Henry Sidgwick** (*The Elements of Politics*), **Harold J. Laski** (*A Grammar of Politics* and *An Introduction to Politics*), **Seymour Lipset** (*Politics and the Social Sciences*), **G.E.G. Catlin** (*Systematic Politics*) and **J.R. Lucas** (*The Principles of Politics*). ❌ No dates are given for any of the five or their works; do not supply them.

⚠️ **The departmental illustration is better than it looks.** The chapter notes that **the University of Bombay has a Department of Civics and Politics while the University of Delhi maintains a Department of Political Science**, and pairs the **Gokhale Institute of Politics and Economics** with the **London School of Economics and Political Science**. ⚠️ The point is that institutional naming is inconsistent, so the terms cannot be told apart by usage — which is why the chapter concludes that **the term 'politics' is often used synonymously with 'political science'**. ⚠️ These are **2009** institutional facts and should be cited as illustrations, not checked against present naming.

### 2. Ball's two problems

> In the first place, 'it is often assumed that politics is only concerned with the public sector, with parliaments, elections, cabinets, and has little relevance to other spheres of human activity'. Secondly, 'there is the danger of confusing politics solely with party politics, that it is somehow concerned with having a political opinion, or that it at least implies a distaste with the intrigues and tricks of party politicians seeking power'.
>
> — Alan Ball, *Modern Politics and Government* (1988), as quoted by Gauba, Ch.3, PDF pp.94-95 (the passage straddles the page break; the extraction prints **contusing**, normalised to **confusing** and catalogued)

⚠️ **The two problems are different in kind, and separating them is the analytical move.**

| Ball's problem | ⚠️ What it gets wrong | ⚠️ Which later lesson answers it |
|---|---|---|
| Politics is **only concerned with the public sector** and **has little relevance to other spheres of human activity** | A mistake about **scope** — it draws the boundary of politics too tightly around formal institutions | Lesson 2, where politics is said to **permeate the entire social fabric** |
| Politics is confused **solely with party politics**, carrying a **distaste** for **intrigues and tricks** | A mistake about **character** — it identifies politics with one of its least attractive manifestations | Lessons 3 to 6, which supply a formal definition in which parties are a late and minor element |

⚠️ ⚠️ **Note the tension the chapter will later have to manage.** ⚠️ Ball's **first** problem says politics is wrongly confined to the public sector — yet Lesson 4 will see the chapter insist, **against Ball**, that only **public** conflicts are political. ⚠️ These are reconcilable: Ball objects to confining politics to the **public sector** meaning formal state institutions, while the chapter's filter is about **public issues** meaning those affecting broad groups. ⚠️ But the words are close enough to trap an unwary answer, and naming the distinction between the **public sector** and **public issues** is worth doing explicitly.

### 3. The catalogue of impressions, and Ernest Benn

✅ **The chapter lists what the layman actually pictures:** **public meetings, processions, slogans, demonstrations, demands, strikes, tear-gas, lathi-charge as well as elections accompanied by false promises and false reports on their fulfilment**.

⚠️ **The list is worth reading as a sequence rather than a heap.** ⚠️ It runs from ordinary participation (meetings, processions, slogans) through contestation (demonstrations, demands, strikes) to **coercion** (tear-gas, lathi-charge) and ends in **deception** (false promises, false reports). ⚠️ So the lay image is not simply negative; it degrades along the way, and the final two items are what generate the verdict.

✅ The verdicts the chapter records: politics gets dubbed **the 'last resort of scoundrels'**; **Ernest Benn** sarcastically described it as **the art of looking for trouble, finding it where it exists or not, diagnosing it wrongly, and applying the wrong remedy**; it is condemned as a **dirty game**; **students are advised to keep away from politics**; and **judges and other intellectuals are expected to keep themselves above politics**.

⚠️ **Benn's definition deserves a moment because it is a four-stage indictment, not a single insult:** manufacturing a problem, misdiagnosing it, and prescribing wrongly. ⚠️ Note that it is introduced as **sarcastic**, so it is reported as a hostile characterisation and not as a position the chapter entertains. ❌ No date or work is given for Benn.

⚠️ **The last two consequences are the most revealing.** ⚠️ If students must keep away and judges must stay above it, then politics is being treated as **contaminating** — which is a strong claim about an activity that, on the chapter's own account, is simply how societies allocate scarce valued things. ⚠️ That gap between the lay verdict and the analytical description is the gap the whole chapter exists to close.

### 4. The programme

✅ **The chapter states its own aim:** for a systematic study it is essential **to dispel such confusion and to lift politics from the arena of vague impressions to the level of scientific precision**.

⚠️ **"Scientific precision" sets the standard the next five lessons meet.** ⚠️ Precision here means supplying **necessary conditions** — what must be present for a situation to be political — rather than listing examples. ⚠️ Lessons 3 to 6 do exactly that, and Lesson 4's rejection of Ball's own example is the clearest evidence that the chapter means the standard seriously: it is willing to correct the authority it has just been quoting.

Revision notes:
- ✅ **The term 'politics' is applied to a particular social phenomenon as well as to a systematic study of that phenomenon.** ⚠️ Activity and discipline; this session is about the **activity**.
- ✅ Aristotle used *Politics* as a title **to indicate a distinct branch of study**. ✅ Five writers named with works: **Sidgwick** (*The Elements of Politics*), **Laski** (*A Grammar of Politics*; *An Introduction to Politics*), **Lipset** (*Politics and the Social Sciences*), **Catlin** (*Systematic Politics*), **Lucas** (*The Principles of Politics*). ❌ No dates given.
- ✅ Departmental illustrations — **Bombay**'s **Civics and Politics** against **Delhi**'s **Political Science**; the **Gokhale Institute of Politics and Economics** against the **London School of Economics and Political Science** — showing that **the term 'politics' is often used synonymously with 'political science'**. ⚠️ **2009** facts.
- ✅ **Ball's two problems**: politics assumed **only concerned with the public sector … little relevance to other spheres**; and confused **solely with party politics**, implying **a distaste with the intrigues and tricks of party politicians seeking power**.
- ⚠️ The first is an error about **scope**, the second about **character**. ⚠️ Ball's "public sector" is not the chapter's later "public issues" — keep them apart.
- ✅ Lay catalogue: **public meetings, processions, slogans, demonstrations, demands, strikes, tear-gas, lathi-charge**, plus elections with **false promises and false reports**.
- ✅ **Last resort of scoundrels**; **Ernest Benn** sarcastically — **the art of looking for trouble, finding it where it exists or not, diagnosing it wrongly, and applying the wrong remedy**; a **dirty game**; students **advised to keep away**; judges and intellectuals expected to keep **above politics**. ❌ No date or work for Benn.
- ✅ The programme: **dispel such confusion** and **lift politics from the arena of vague impressions to the level of scientific precision**.

### Concept check

**Question:** Ball's first problem is that politics is wrongly assumed to be "only concerned with the public sector". Yet the chapter later insists that only **public** conflicts are political. Is the chapter contradicting the authority it quotes?

**Model answer:** No, because two different senses of "public" are in play and the chapter's filter is not the one Ball attacks. Ball's objection is to confining politics to the **public sector** — a set of **institutions**, namely **parliaments, elections, cabinets** — with the consequence that politics is thought to have **little relevance to other spheres of human activity**. ⚠️ The chapter endorses that objection fully, concluding at Lesson 2 that politics **does not operate strictly within the framework of an institutional set-up but permeates the entire social fabric**. ⚠️ The chapter's own filter, by contrast, is about **public issues**: a political conflict must arise **at a 'public' level**, meaning one **in which two or more major groups are involved**, and it must demand **a 'universal' solution, not a private one**. ⚠️ That is a test of **who is affected**, not of **where the conflict occurs**, which is why the chapter can say that a conflict between **husband and wife** becomes political once it takes the form of **respective claims of men and women** demanding **solution at the public level** — a conflict located entirely outside the public sector that nonetheless passes the public-issue test. ⚠️ The two positions are therefore complementary: Ball widens the **locations** of politics, and the chapter narrows the **kinds of conflict** that count, and an answer that notices the chapter correcting the authority it quotes has read the page rather than skimmed it.

**Misconception to avoid:** Do not report Ernest Benn's definition as the chapter's view of politics. It is introduced as a **sarcastic** description and sits inside the catalogue of lay impressions the chapter is assembling precisely in order to **dispel** them. The chapter's own stated aim on the same page is to **lift politics from the arena of vague impressions to the level of scientific precision**, which is the opposite of endorsing Benn.

## Lesson 2 — From *polis* to social process

Progress: 2 / 24 | Stage: Foundation | Subtopic: The etymology, the traditional programme, and the relocation of the subject

🖼️ **VISUAL — a derivation, a programme, and a break with it**

```text

   Greek 'POLIS' = "ancient Greek city-state"
        |
   "the activities of citizens IN THEIR ROLE AS MEMBERS AND
    OPERATORS OF THE STATE were termed by ancient Greek thinkers
    as 'politics'"
        |
   => "from the very beginning, 'politics' came to be ASSOCIATED

                              |

    'SCIENCE OF THE STATE', and have devoted themselves:"

     (b) "to developing IDEAS concerning the nature of a
          PERFECT STATE"                                  -> normative
                              |
   THE BREAK
   "HOWEVER, modern writers have increasingly recognized that
    'politics' DOES NOT OPERATE STRICTLY WITHIN THE FRAMEWORK OF
    AN INSTITUTIONAL SET-UP but PERMEATES THE ENTIRE SOCIAL
    FABRIC."
                              |
   THE RELOCATION
   "Accordingly, 'politics' is now treated as a SOCIAL PROCESS
    rather than an aggregate of the formal institutions of the
    state."
                              |
   THE QUESTION THAT FOLLOWS
   "How does this process operate?"   -> Lessons 3 to 11
```

*Caption: the chapter concedes the state-centred origin of the word and then detaches the subject from it. Everything after this page is an attempt to say what politics is **without** defining it as whatever happens inside state institutions.*

### 1. The derivation, and what it fixed

> The word 'politics' itself is derived from the Greek word 'polis' which denoted ancient Greek city-state. The activities of citizens in their role as members and operators of the state were termed by ancient Greek thinkers as 'politics'. Thus, from the very beginning, 'politics' came to be associated with the 'state'.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.3, PDF p.95 (reproduced as the chapter's wording; the extraction prints **polities**, normalised to **politics** and catalogued)

⚠️ **Two features of the Greek formulation carry forward.** ⚠️ First, it is about **citizens** — a bounded category, which anticipates Lesson 7's point that Greek politicization **excluded the very large majority of slaves and aliens**. ⚠️ Second, it is about citizens **in their role as members and operators of the state**, so it is **role-defined**: the same person is political in one capacity and not in another. ⚠️ That role-relativity survives even after the state-centred framing is abandoned, and it is what makes Lesson 9's husband-and-wife transformation intelligible.

⚠️ ⚠️ **Note the honesty of "from the very beginning".** ⚠️ The chapter is not pretending the state-link was an error that crept in; it concedes that the association is as old as the word. ⚠️ That makes the break at §3 a genuine revision rather than a correction, and an answer that presents the traditional view as simply mistaken has misread the move.

### 2. The traditional programme

✅ **Traditional writers** considered political science **the 'science of the state'** and devoted themselves to **(a) a study of the institutions of the state** and **(b) developing ideas concerning the nature of a perfect state**.

⚠️ **These two tasks are of different kinds and the pairing is significant.** ⚠️ Task (a) is **descriptive** — what the institutions are and how they work. Task (b) is **normative** — what a state ought to be. ⚠️ The traditional discipline therefore contained both an empirical and an evaluative wing from the outset, which is worth remembering because ➡️ the behaviouralist attempt to strip the second away belongs to the next chapter and its owner, and Topic 01 recorded the same two-wing structure for political theory generally.

⚠️ **What both tasks share is their object: the state.** ⚠️ That is the common feature the relocation attacks — not the descriptive-normative pairing, which survives, but the restriction of both to a single institution.

### 3. The break, and the relocation

> However, modern writers have increasingly recognized that 'politics' does not operate strictly within the framework of an institutional set-up but permeates the entire social fabric. Accordingly, 'politics' is now treated as a social process rather than an aggregate of the formal institutions of the state.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.3, PDF p.95 (reproduced as the chapter's wording)

⚠️ ⚠️ **This sentence is the hinge of the entire chapter and should be learned verbatim in substance.** ⚠️ It replaces an **aggregate** with a **process** — a thing you could list with a thing you must trace. ⚠️ Institutions can be enumerated; a process has to be followed through whatever material it passes through, which is precisely what **permeates the entire social fabric** asserts.

⚠️ **Three consequences follow, and each is cashed out later in the chapter.**

| Consequence of the relocation | Where the chapter cashes it out |
|---|---|
| Politics can occur **outside** formal institutions | Lesson 9's husband-and-wife case, which becomes political without entering any institution |
| The boundary of politics must be drawn by a **criterion**, not by a list of bodies | Lessons 3 to 6, which supply conflict, publicness, non-war and authoritativeness |
| Politics is a **part** of something larger and interacts with its neighbours | Lesson 12, where the political system is a **sub-system of the larger social system** and yields **political economy** and **political culture** |

⚠️ ⚠️ **But the relocation also creates the chapter's central problem, and naming it is a strong move.** ⚠️ Once politics is no longer identified with the state, what stops **everything** from being political? ⚠️ A process that **permeates the entire social fabric** has no obvious edge. ⚠️ Lessons 3 and 4 are the chapter's answer — it must now **manufacture** a boundary that the state-centred definition supplied for free — and that is why it is willing to reject the example of the very authority it is relying on.

⚠️ **The chapter signals that it knows this.** ⚠️ It closes the paragraph with a question rather than a claim: **How does this process operate?** ⚠️ The remainder of the chapter's first half is the answer.

Revision notes:
- ✅ **'Politics' is derived from the Greek word 'polis' which denoted ancient Greek city-state**; the **activities of citizens in their role as members and operators of the state** were termed politics, so **from the very beginning, 'politics' came to be associated with the 'state'**.
- ⚠️ Two carried-forward features: it concerns **citizens** (a bounded category), and it is **role-defined**.
- ✅ Traditional writers treated political science as the **science of the state**, studying **(a) the institutions of the state** and **(b) ideas concerning the nature of a perfect state** — ⚠️ one descriptive wing, one normative wing, both fixed on one object.
- ✅ The break: politics **does not operate strictly within the framework of an institutional set-up but permeates the entire social fabric**.
- ✅ The relocation: politics **is now treated as a social process rather than an aggregate of the formal institutions of the state**.
- ⚠️ An **aggregate** can be listed; a **process** must be traced. That is the whole difference.
- ⚠️ The relocation's cost: it removes the ready-made boundary the state supplied, which is why Lessons 3-6 must build one.
- ✅ The chapter closes with a question: **How does this process operate?**

### Concept check

**Question:** What problem does the chapter create for itself by relocating politics from the state to "a social process", and how does the rest of the chapter solve it?

**Model answer:** It creates a **boundary problem**. While politics was the **science of the state**, its subject matter was fixed by an institution: whatever happened in or to the state was political, and nothing else was. ⚠️ Once the chapter accepts that politics **does not operate strictly within the framework of an institutional set-up but permeates the entire social fabric**, that free boundary disappears, and a process said to permeate everything risks including everything — which would make the concept useless. ⚠️ The chapter solves it by replacing an institutional boundary with a set of **criteria**, built across the next four pages. It requires a **conflict** together with **efforts for the resolution of that conflict through the authority of government**; it requires the conflict to be **public**, meaning that **two or more major groups are involved** and that it **demands a 'universal' solution, not a private one**; it excludes **war**, since **war is a symptom of the failure of a political solution**; and with Easton it requires the settlement to be **authoritative**, resting on **the capacity to secure more-or-less willing compliance** rather than on **brute force**. ⚠️ The decisive evidence that the chapter takes the problem seriously is that it rejects **Alan Ball's own example** of two children and a toy — correcting the authority it has been quoting rather than accepting an example that would have left the boundary undrawn. ⚠️ An answer that states the problem before the solution shows why the apparently pedantic filters of Lessons 3 and 4 are doing necessary work.

**Misconception to avoid:** Do not present the traditional **science of the state** programme as merely descriptive or as a crude error. The chapter credits it with **two** tasks — studying **the institutions of the state** and **developing ideas concerning the nature of a perfect state** — so it already contained both an empirical and a normative wing. What the relocation rejects is not that pairing but the restriction of both wings to a single institution.

## Lesson 3 — The political situation

Progress: 3 / 24 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: Ball, Wasby and Miller, and the two-element formula the chapter extracts

Book context: Canonical Core owner §§3 and 4 with the Advanced companion's §3 step 2, read against the source chapter's section headed "The Political Situation" (Gauba, Ch.3, PDF pp.95-96), which supply Ball's conflict-and-resolution formula, Wasby's controversy formulation, Miller's addition of government and the chapter's own summary of the political process.
CA search: Not re-run for this lesson; see Lesson 1.
CA found: As reported there.
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

🖼️ **VISUAL — three authorities, each adding one element**

```text
   "Politics as a process operates in a particular situation which
    may be termed as 'POLITICAL SITUATION'."              (p.95)
                              |
   AUTHORITY 1  ALAN BALL (Modern Politics and Government; 1988)
   "political activity ... involves DISAGREEMENTS and the
    RECONCILIATION of those disagreements"
   "the essence of the political situation" is "CONFLICT AND
    RESOLUTION OF THAT CONFLICT"
        -> ELEMENT A: conflict        ELEMENT B: its resolution
                              |
   AUTHORITY 2  STEPHEN L. WASBY (Political Science -- The
   Discipline and its Dimensions: An Introduction; 1972)
   "Where there is politics, it is said, there is CONTROVERSY;
    where there are ISSUES, there is politics. Where NO
    CONTROVERSY exists, where NO ISSUES are being debated,
    POLITICS DOES NOT EXIST."
        -> states the condition BOTH WAYS: a BICONDITIONAL.
           No controversy => no politics. The absence clause is
           what makes it a test rather than a description.
                              |
   AUTHORITY 3  J.D.B. MILLER (The Nature of Politics; 1962)
   "Political activity ... arises out of disagreement, and it is
    concerned with the USE OF GOVERNMENT to resolve conflict in
    the direction of CHANGE or in the PREVENTION of change ...
    Politics ... demands some initial disagreement between
    parties or persons, and THE PRESENCE OF GOVERNMENT as a
    means of resolving the disagreement in some direction."
        -> ELEMENT C: government, as the MEANS
        -> and note: change OR the PREVENTION of change -- both
           directions are political
                              |
   THE CHAPTER'S OWN SUMMARY                                 (p.96)
   "the political process postulates the existence of a
    DISAGREEMENT OR CONFLICT, and efforts for the RESOLUTION of
    that conflict THROUGH THE AUTHORITY OF GOVERNMENT"
                              |
   AND THE WARNING THAT OPENS LESSON 4
   "Thus, EVERY situation involving a conflict and efforts for its
    resolution CANNOT be called a 'political situation'."
```

*Caption: three quotations, three elements — conflict, resolution, and government as the means. The chapter's own summary keeps all three and then immediately warns that they are **not yet sufficient**.*

### 1. Ball: conflict and resolution

> According to Alan Ball (Modern Politics and Government; 1988), 'political activity . . . involves disagreements and the reconciliation of those disagreements'. In other words, 'the essence of the political situation' is 'conflict and resolution of that conflict'.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.3, PDF p.95 (reproduced as the chapter's wording; the ellipsis is the chapter's)

⚠️ **Two elements, and both are required.** ⚠️ Disagreement alone is not politics — it is merely difference. ⚠️ Resolution alone is not politics either, since plenty of things are settled without any prior conflict. ⚠️ It is the **pairing** that is essential, and the word **essence** signals that Ball is offering a definition rather than a description.

### 2. Wasby: the biconditional

> Where there is politics, it is said, there is controversy; where there are issues, there is politics. Where no controversy exists, where no issues are being debated, politics does not exist.
>
> — Stephen L. Wasby, *Political Science—The Discipline and its Dimensions: An Introduction* (1972), as quoted by Gauba, Ch.3, PDF p.95

⚠️ ⚠️ **Wasby's formulation is more useful than Ball's because it runs in both directions**, and noticing that is worth marks. ⚠️ The first half states that politics implies controversy and that issues imply politics. ⚠️ The **second** half — **where no controversy exists … politics does not exist** — is the one that makes the formula a **test**: it tells you what to look for in order to rule a case **out**. ⚠️ A definition that only tells you what politics involves cannot exclude anything; a biconditional can.

⚠️ **Note the hedge "it is said".** ⚠️ Wasby is reporting a received view rather than announcing a discovery, which is consistent with the chapter's treatment of all three authorities as converging on something already broadly accepted.

### 3. Miller: government as the means

> Political activity . . . arises out of disagreement, and it is concerned with the use of government to resolve conflict in the direction of change or in the prevention of change ... Politics, to be distinguished as a recognizable activity, demands some initial disagreement between parties or persons, and the presence of government as a means of resolving the disagreement in some direction.
>
> — J.D.B. Miller, *The Nature of Politics* (1962), as quoted by Gauba, Ch.3, PDF pp.95-96 (the passage straddles the page break; the ellipses are the chapter's)

⚠️ **Miller adds the third element and one important symmetry.**

⚠️ **The third element is government**, named twice — as what politics is **concerned with the use of**, and as **the presence of government as a means of resolving**. ⚠️ Note the careful phrasing: government is the **means**, not the **arena**. ⚠️ That matters, because after Lesson 2's relocation politics is no longer defined by where it happens; Miller's formula lets government back in as an **instrument** without reinstating it as a **location**.

⚠️ ⚠️ **The symmetry is the under-noticed part: "in the direction of change or in the prevention of change".** ⚠️ Both are political. ⚠️ Resisting a proposal, defending an existing arrangement and blocking a reform are political acts exactly as much as demanding reform is. ⚠️ An answer that treats politics as inherently about change has missed half of Miller's formula — and this symmetry is what allows Lesson 17's Marxist account, in which politics functions to **suppress** conflict and hold an order in place, to count as politics at all rather than as its absence.

### 4. The chapter's own summary, and the warning

> In a nutshell, the political process postulates the existence of a disagreement or conflict, and efforts for the resolution of that conflict through the authority of government. Thus, every situation involving a conflict and efforts for its resolution cannot be called a 'political situation'.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.3, PDF p.96 (reproduced as the chapter's wording)

⚠️ **The summary keeps all three elements and upgrades one.** ⚠️ Miller's **use of government** becomes **the authority of government** — and **authority** is the word Lesson 5's Easton will unpack at length. ⚠️ The chapter is quietly introducing its own next move inside its summary of someone else's.

⚠️ ⚠️ **The second sentence is the most important in the lesson.** ⚠️ Having assembled a three-element formula from three authorities, the chapter immediately declares it **insufficient**: **every situation involving a conflict and efforts for its resolution cannot be called a 'political situation'**. ⚠️ That is an unusual and honest move — it says the received account under-determines the concept — and it sets up Lesson 4, where two further filters are added **against** the example Ball himself supplies.

Revision notes:
- ✅ **Politics as a process operates in a particular situation which may be termed as 'political situation'.**
- ✅ **Alan Ball** (*Modern Politics and Government*; **1988**): political activity **involves disagreements and the reconciliation of those disagreements**; **the essence of the political situation** is **conflict and resolution of that conflict**.
- ✅ **Stephen L. Wasby** (*Political Science—The Discipline and its Dimensions: An Introduction*; **1972**): **where there are issues, there is politics. Where no controversy exists, where no issues are being debated, politics does not exist.**
- ⚠️ Wasby's value is the **absence clause** — it makes the formula a test that can rule cases **out**.
- ✅ **J.D.B. Miller** (*The Nature of Politics*; **1962**): politics **arises out of disagreement**, concerns **the use of government to resolve conflict in the direction of change or in the prevention of change**, and demands **the presence of government as a means of resolving the disagreement in some direction**.
- ⚠️ Government is the **means**, not the arena. ⚠️ **Change or the prevention of change** — both directions are political, which is what lets the Marxist suppression account count as politics.
- ✅ Chapter's summary: the political process **postulates the existence of a disagreement or conflict, and efforts for the resolution of that conflict through the authority of government** — ⚠️ note the upgrade from **use** to **authority**.
- ✅ And the warning: **every situation involving a conflict and efforts for its resolution cannot be called a 'political situation'**.

### Concept check

**Question:** Miller says politics concerns the use of government "in the direction of change or in the prevention of change". Why does that symmetry matter for the rest of the chapter?

**Model answer:** Because without it two of the chapter's three rival theories would fall outside its own definition of politics. ⚠️ If politics were defined as activity directed at **change**, then the Marxist account could not count, since on that account politics is what the dominant class uses to **suppress the conflict** — to hold an existing order in place against pressure — and the chapter says so explicitly: **politics is used by the dominant class to suppress the conflict**, producing **a compromise imposed by the dominant class on the dependent class** and even **an 'illusion of consent'**. ⚠️ Miller's symmetry makes that activity political by definition rather than a failure of politics. ⚠️ The same applies to the conservative possibility within the liberal account, where the **red and green signal system** or a **queue system** exists precisely to **prevent** disruptive change by stabilising expectations. ⚠️ The symmetry also protects the chapter from a subtler error: if only change were political, then a settled, satisfied society would be apolitical, whereas on Miller's account the continuous prevention of change is itself a political achievement requiring the use of government. ⚠️ A good answer closes by noting that the symmetry is what makes Lesson 22's scope-conditions verdict coherent, since a theory can be judged by how well it explains the **holding** of an order as well as its transformation.

**Misconception to avoid:** Do not treat the three quotations as three competing definitions to be chosen between. The chapter stacks them: Ball supplies **conflict and resolution of that conflict**, Wasby supplies the absence clause that converts this into a test, and Miller adds **the presence of government as a means**. The chapter's own summary retains all three elements, and then says they are still **not sufficient** — which is the point of Lesson 4, not a rejection of any one authority.

*Caption: the chapter corrects the authority it has been quoting. That is the clearest evidence that its filters are doing real work rather than decorating a received definition.*

### 1. The example, and why the chapter takes it on

✅ The chapter quotes Ball's illustration in full and then rules: **this example suffers from two defects**.

⚠️ ⚠️ **Rejecting your own principal authority's illustration is a strong move and an answer should say so.** ⚠️ Ball supplied the conflict-and-resolution formula at Lesson 3 and his *Modern Politics and Government* (1988) is cited twice on the preceding page. ⚠️ The chapter accepts the formula and rejects the example — which is only possible because it is adding criteria Ball's formula does not contain. ⚠️ The correction is therefore the chapter's own contribution, not a reproduction of received doctrine.

### 2. Defect 1 — the conflict is private

> In the first place, it refers to a 'private' conflict between two children which does not qualify for being a political situation. Similarly, a disagreement between husband and wife on the family budget does not present a political situation. Only that conflict can be considered the proper subject of politics which arises at a 'public' level, i.e. in which two or more major groups are involved in any social setting, whether local, regional, national or international, or at the economic, cultural, linguistic, religious or ethnic plane. In other words, a political conflict is always concerned with 'public' issues, not private issues; it demands a 'universal' solution, not a private one.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.3, PDF p.96 (reproduced as the chapter's wording)

⚠️ **The criterion has three parts and all three are needed.**

| Part | What it requires | ⚠️ What it excludes |
|---|---|---|
| **Two or more major groups** | The parties must be **groups**, and **major** ones | Individual quarrels, however bitter — the children, the spouses |
| **Any social setting, whether local, regional, national or international** | ⚠️ Scale is **not** a criterion — a local conflict qualifies | ⚠️ The common error that only national politics is politics |
| **Or at the economic, cultural, linguistic, religious or ethnic plane** | ⚠️ Subject matter is **not** a criterion either | ⚠️ The error that only economic or constitutional matters are political |

⚠️ ⚠️ **Note what the two lists do: they widen, not narrow.** ⚠️ Having restricted politics to public conflicts, the chapter immediately insists that **public** has nothing to do with scale or with subject matter. ⚠️ A village dispute over a cultural practice is as political as a treaty negotiation, provided major groups are involved and a universal solution is demanded. ⚠️ That is a substantial claim and it is easy to miss because it sits inside a sentence whose main clause is restrictive.

⚠️ **And note the second formulation, which is the sharper test:** it **demands a 'universal' solution, not a private one**. ⚠️ This is a test on the **remedy**, not on the parties. ⚠️ A conflict is political if settling it requires a rule that applies to everyone in the relevant class — which is exactly why Lesson 9's husband-and-wife case can convert: the quarrel stays between two people, but once it is posed as **respective claims of men and women**, the remedy must be universal.

### 3. The church, university and factory caution

✅ **In common parlance, we talk of the politics of church, university or a factory. But as long as this so-called politics is confined to a single organization with little effect on public life, it cannot be considered the proper subject of study in political science.**

⚠️ **This is the chapter guarding its new boundary from the other side.** ⚠️ Lesson 2 widened politics beyond state institutions; this sentence stops the widening from swallowing every organisation's internal manoeuvring. ⚠️ Note the **two** conditions joined: **confined to a single organization** **and** **with little effect on public life**. ⚠️ Both must hold for the exclusion to apply, so organisational politics that **does** affect public life is not excluded.

⚠️ ❌ **Scan-artefact disclosure.** The extraction prints **co-called**, normalised to **so-called** and catalogued.

### 4. Defect 2 — violence and war

> In the second place, Alan Ball's example contemplates 'resort to violence' as one of the possible methods of conflict-resolution. But, properly speaking, resort to violence or war does not qualify for a political solution. No doubt, some political disputes do culminate in war, but war is a symptom of the failure of a political solution. On the contrary, a political solution emanates from negotiation, persuasion, arbitration, compromise, pressure, counting of votes or any similar tactic short of war. In any case, the solution is supposed to be acceptable to the parties involved in the dispute.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.3, PDF p.96 (reproduced as the chapter's wording)

⚠️ ⚠️ **"War is a symptom of the failure of a political solution" is the most quotable line in the first half of the chapter.** ⚠️ It is carefully built: it **concedes** that **some political disputes do culminate in war** — so war is not unrelated to politics — while denying that war is itself a political **solution**. ⚠️ War is the **failure mode** of politics, which is a different relation from being an instance of it.

✅ **Six qualifying methods are named**, plus a catch-all: **negotiation, persuasion, arbitration, compromise, pressure, counting of votes or any similar tactic short of war**.

⚠️ **The list repays sorting, because its members are not of one kind.**

| Method | ⚠️ What it relies on |
|---|---|
| **Negotiation**, **compromise** | Mutual adjustment between the parties themselves |
| **Persuasion** | Change of mind — the only method that alters what a party **wants** |
| **Arbitration** | A third party, which is Ball's own **mother** in the rejected example |
| **Pressure** | ⚠️ Unequal leverage short of force — the method the Marxist account at Lesson 17 says is doing the real work throughout |
| **Counting of votes** | A procedure that converts disagreement into a determinate outcome without resolving it |

⚠️ ⚠️ **"Pressure" is the interesting inclusion.** ⚠️ It is not consensual in the way negotiation is, yet the chapter counts it as political because it stops **short of war**. ⚠️ That is precisely the gap Lesson 21's Miliband material exploits: a settlement reached under pressure satisfies the chapter's criterion while leaving the parties in a relation of **dominance** and **dependence**. ⚠️ Naming that opening here, and cashing it in at Lesson 21, is how a 20-mark answer shows it has read the chapter as a connected argument.

⚠️ **The acceptability clause is the chapter's bridge to Easton.** ⚠️ **The solution is supposed to be acceptable to the parties involved in the dispute** raises the obvious question — how is acceptability secured when one side has lost? ⚠️ The chapter asks it directly: **How is this made possible?** ⚠️ Lesson 5 answers with **authority**.

Revision notes:
- ✅ Ball's example — **two children in a nursery with one toy** who could **resort to violence** or have **the mother … arbitrate** — **suffers from two defects**.
- ⚠️ The chapter rejects its own principal authority's illustration; that is its own contribution, not received doctrine.
- ✅ **Defect 1:** it is a **'private' conflict** which **does not qualify**; likewise **a disagreement between husband and wife on the family budget**.
- ✅ The test: conflict must arise **at a 'public' level, i.e. in which two or more major groups are involved in any social setting, whether local, regional, national or international, or at the economic, cultural, linguistic, religious or ethnic plane**; it is concerned with **'public' issues, not private issues** and **demands a 'universal' solution, not a private one**.
- ⚠️ Scale and subject matter are explicitly **not** criteria — both lists widen. ⚠️ The sharper test is on the **remedy**: does it have to be universal?
- ✅ Caution: the **politics of church, university or a factory** is excluded **as long as** it is **confined to a single organization** **with little effect on public life** — ⚠️ both conditions required.
- ✅ **Defect 2:** **resort to violence or war does not qualify for a political solution**; **some political disputes do culminate in war, but war is a symptom of the failure of a political solution**.
- ✅ Qualifying methods: **negotiation, persuasion, arbitration, compromise, pressure, counting of votes or any similar tactic short of war**.
- ⚠️ **Pressure** is the opening Lesson 21 exploits — a settlement under pressure meets the criterion while leaving dominance intact.
- ✅ **The solution is supposed to be acceptable to the parties involved in the dispute** — and the chapter asks **How is this made possible?**

### Concept check

**Question:** The chapter says war "is a symptom of the failure of a political solution". How is that different from saying war is not political, and why does the distinction matter?

**Model answer:** It is different because a symptom of failure stands in a definite relation to the thing that failed, whereas being non-political would place war outside the subject altogether. ⚠️ The chapter is explicit that the relation holds: **some political disputes do culminate in war**, so war arises **out of** political disputes and is intelligible only by reference to them. ⚠️ What it denies is narrower — that **resort to violence or war does not qualify for a political solution**, that is, war is not one of the methods by which politics does its work. ⚠️ The distinction matters for three reasons. First, it keeps war inside the explanatory scope of political science as an **outcome** to be explained, while excluding it from the **definition** of the political process — which is why a chapter that excludes war can still speak of conflicts **at the international plane**. Second, it supplies a criterion of **failure**, and a concept with a defined failure mode is sharper than one without, since we can now ask of any settlement whether it succeeded on politics' own terms. Third, it coheres with the acceptability clause, since **the solution is supposed to be acceptable to the parties involved in the dispute** and a settlement imposed by force secures compliance without acceptability — which is exactly the gap Easton fills at Lesson 5 by distinguishing **authority** from **brute force**. ⚠️ A complete answer notes the residual honestly: **pressure** is listed among the qualifying methods, so the line between acceptable settlement and imposed outcome is less clean than the war exclusion suggests, and Lesson 21's Miliband material presses precisely there.

**Misconception to avoid:** Do not read the public/private filter as a statement about scale or subject matter. The chapter's own sentence widens on both axes — a qualifying conflict may arise in **any social setting, whether local, regional, national or international**, and **at the economic, cultural, linguistic, religious or ethnic plane**. What makes a conflict public is that **two or more major groups** are involved and that it **demands a 'universal' solution**, not that it is large or that it concerns government business.

## Lesson 5 — Easton: the authoritative allocation of values

🖼️ **VISUAL — three words, each with its own gloss**

```text

   |              |                ALTERNATIVES"                 |
   |              |   POLICY   = "arriving at a decision AS WELL |
   |              |                AS ITS IMPLEMENTATION"        |
   +--------------+----------------------------------------------+
   | AUTHORITATIVE| "a policy is authoritative when the people   |
   |              |  to whom it is intended to apply or who are  |
   |              |  affected by it consider that THEY MUST OR   |
   |              |  OUGHT TO OBEY IT"                           |
   |              |                                              |
   |              | AND THE NEGATIVE DEFINITION:                 |
   |              | "AUTHORITY DOES NOT SIGNIFY THE USE OF BRUTE |
   |              |  FORCE; it denotes the CAPACITY TO SECURE    |
   |              |  MORE-OR-LESS WILLING COMPLIANCE from its    |
   |              |  subjects for a particular decision or       |
   |              |  course of action"                           |
   +--------------+----------------------------------------------+
                              |
   THE UNIVERSALITY CLAIM, in Easton's own words:
   "EVERY SOCIETY provides SOME MECHANISMS, however rudimentary
    they may be, for authoritatively resolving differences about
    the ends that are to be pursued, that is for deciding WHO IS
    TO GET WHAT there is of desirable things. An authoritative
    allocation of some values is UNAVOIDABLE."
                              |
   WHAT IT ADDS: "It brings in the element of AUTHORITATIVENESS to
   the process of conflict-resolution."
```

*Caption: the definition is three words long and the chapter spends two pages on it. Each term is glossed twice — once positively and, for authority, once negatively against brute force.*

### 1. The definition and its first term

> According to David Easton (The Political System: An Inquiry into the State of Political Science; 1953), politics is concerned with the 'authoritative allocation of values' for a society. This short but compact definition involves three terms which must be understood clearly. These are: 'values', 'allocation', and 'authoritative'. By 'values' he means the 'things considered valuable, whether they be spiritual or material'.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.3, PDF p.96 (reproduced as the chapter's wording)

⚠️ **The gloss on "values" is doing anti-reductionist work.** ⚠️ **Whether they be spiritual or material** blocks the reading that politics is only about the distribution of goods and money. ⚠️ Lesson 8 will list **power, enlightenment, wealth, well-being, health, skill, affection, rectitude and deference** — a list in which several items are plainly not material. ⚠️ An answer that treats "values" here as "moral values" has also gone wrong: the word means **things considered valuable**, which is a wider and more neutral category.

⚠️ **Note "for a society".** ⚠️ The allocation is society-wide, which is Easton's own version of Lesson 4's public-issue filter. ⚠️ The two criteria converge from different directions.

### 2. Allocation, policy and decision

✅ **By allocation** Easton means **distribution of these things to various individuals or groups**, and this is **accomplished through policy which consists of a 'web of decisions'**. ✅ **Decision** denotes the **selection among alternatives**; **policy implies arriving at a decision as well as its implementation**.

⚠️ ⚠️ **Three nested concepts, and the nesting matters.**

| Level | Definition | ⚠️ What distinguishes it from the level below |
|---|---|---|
| **Allocation** | **Distribution … to various individuals or groups** | The outcome — who ends up with what |
| **Policy** | **Arriving at a decision as well as its implementation** | ⚠️ Adds **implementation**; a decision that is never implemented allocates nothing |
| **Decision** | **Selection among alternatives** | The minimal unit — a choice |

⚠️ **The phrase "web of decisions" is the one to retain.** ⚠️ It says that a policy is not a single act but a **connected set** of choices, which is why allocation is accomplished through policy rather than through decisions directly. ⚠️ And the insistence that policy includes **implementation** is what makes the definition empirically testable: an allocation that exists only on paper has not been made.

### 3. Authority, defined positively and negatively

> a policy is authoritative when the people to whom it is intended to apply or who are affected by it consider that they must or ought to obey it … authority does not signify the use of brute force; it denotes the capacity to secure more-or-less willing compliance from its subjects for a particular decision or course of action.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.3, PDF p.97 (reproduced as the chapter's wording; the ellipsis replaces the chapter's intervening phrase)

⚠️ ⚠️ **Three features of this gloss repay attention, and all three are examinable.**

1. ⚠️ **Authority is defined by the subjects' attitude, not by the ruler's capacity.** A policy is authoritative **when the people … consider that they must or ought to obey it**. ⚠️ That locates authority in the **belief of the governed**, which is a striking place to put it and is what distinguishes it from power.
2. ⚠️ **The disjunction "must or ought" is not redundant.** **Must** is compulsion felt as necessity; **ought** is obligation felt as duty. ⚠️ Lesson 10 will cash this out, where Easton allows a policy to be accepted **on several grounds: 'moral, traditional or customary, or purely from fear of consequences'** — so **ought** covers the moral and customary grounds and **must** covers fear.
3. ⚠️ **The negative definition is the sharp edge.** **Authority does not signify the use of brute force.** ⚠️ This completes the exclusion begun at Lesson 4: war is excluded as a method, and now brute force is excluded as a basis. ⚠️ What replaces it is **the capacity to secure more-or-less willing compliance** — and the hedge **more-or-less** is doing real work, since it concedes that compliance is rarely wholehearted without conceding that it is coerced.

⚠️ ⚠️ **But hold the tension rather than resolving it.** ⚠️ If a policy can be authoritative **purely from fear of consequences** (Lesson 10), and authority **does not signify the use of brute force**, then fear-based compliance must be distinguished from force-based compliance. ⚠️ The chapter does not draw that line explicitly. ⚠️ Lesson 10 shows it coming closer, since authority there involves **'legitimacy' or 'power' or both**, and is backed by **police, magistrates and prisons**. ⚠️ Naming this as an unresolved tension is better than pretending the chapter has settled it.

### 4. The universality claim

> Every society provides some mechanisms, however rudimentary they may be, for authoritatively resolving differences about the ends that are to be pursued, that is for deciding who is to get what there is of desirable things. An authoritative allocation of some values is unavoidable.
>
> — David Easton, as quoted by Gauba, Ch.3, PDF p.97

⚠️ **This is a strong claim and the chapter endorses it**, saying that politics so defined **represents a universal social phenomenon**.

⚠️ **Three hedges keep it defensible.** ⚠️ **Some mechanisms** — not any particular institution. ⚠️ **However rudimentary they may be** — not necessarily a state, which is what makes the claim compatible with Lesson 2's relocation. ⚠️ **Some values** — not all values need be allocated authoritatively.

⚠️ ⚠️ **And notice what the universality claim does to the chapter's later argument.** ⚠️ If an authoritative allocation **is unavoidable**, then politics cannot simply be abolished — which puts Easton in direct tension with Lesson 17's Marxist conclusion that **the end of class-conflict marks an end of politics itself**. ⚠️ The chapter does not stage that confrontation, and an answer that stages it has found a genuine disagreement **inside** the chapter rather than importing one.

✅ **What Easton adds, in the chapter's own summary:** his observations **add a new dimension to our earlier formulation regarding the nature of politics. It brings in the element of authoritativeness to the process of conflict-resolution.**

Revision notes:
- ✅ **David Easton** (*The Political System: An Inquiry into the State of Political Science*; **1953**): politics is concerned with the **authoritative allocation of values** for a society — **three terms which must be understood clearly**.
- ✅ **Values** = **the things considered valuable, whether they be spiritual or material** — ⚠️ not only material, and not "moral values".
- ✅ **Allocation** = **distribution of these things to various individuals or groups**, **accomplished through policy which consists of a 'web of decisions'**.
- ✅ **Decision** = **the selection among alternatives**; **policy implies arriving at a decision as well as its implementation** — ⚠️ implementation is part of the definition.
- ✅ **Authoritative**: a policy is authoritative **when the people to whom it is intended to apply or who are affected by it consider that they must or ought to obey it**.
- ✅ Negatively: **authority does not signify the use of brute force; it denotes the capacity to secure more-or-less willing compliance from its subjects for a particular decision or course of action**.
- ⚠️ Authority sits in the **subjects' belief**, not the ruler's capacity. ⚠️ **Must** covers fear, **ought** covers moral and customary grounds (see Lesson 10).
- ⚠️ Unresolved: if fear of consequences can ground authority, how is that distinguished from brute force? The chapter does not say.
- ✅ Universality: **every society provides some mechanisms, however rudimentary they may be, for authoritatively resolving differences about the ends that are to be pursued, that is for deciding who is to get what there is of desirable things. An authoritative allocation of some values is unavoidable.**
- ⚠️ Three hedges: **some mechanisms**, **however rudimentary**, **some values**. ⚠️ The claim sits in tension with the Marxist end-of-politics thesis at Lesson 17.
- ✅ Easton **brings in the element of authoritativeness to the process of conflict-resolution**.

### Concept check

**Question:** Easton says an authoritative allocation of some values is "unavoidable". The Marxist view says the end of class conflict marks the end of politics. Can both be held? Work out what each would have to concede.

**Model answer:** Not without one of them yielding, and identifying exactly what would have to yield is the valuable part. Easton's claim is strong and general: **every society provides some mechanisms, however rudimentary they may be, for authoritatively resolving differences about the ends that are to be pursued**, and such allocation **is unavoidable** — a claim grounded in scarcity and plurality of ends rather than in class structure. ⚠️ The Marxist conclusion is equally unqualified: with the elimination of classes **state and politics would no longer be required; the state will 'wither away'**, and **the end of class-conflict marks an end of politics itself**. ⚠️ For both to hold, the Marxist would have to concede that what ends is **class politics** rather than allocation as such, leaving some residual mechanism for deciding **who is to get what there is of desirable things** in a classless society — a concession that weakens the withering-away thesis to a claim about the state rather than about politics. ⚠️ Alternatively Easton would have to concede that his universality claim is an induction from class-divided societies and need not hold of a society without scarcity-driven antagonism — but his grounds are **differences about the ends that are to be pursued**, which plurality of ends alone can generate without any class division. ⚠️ A complete answer notes that the chapter never stages this confrontation, so identifying it is a reading of the text rather than a report of it; and that the chapter's own conclusion sides, in effect, with Easton, since it records that **the dream of a 'classless' society is extremely difficult to realize** and that **conditions of 'dominance' and 'dependence' exist in society at many subtle levels**.

**Misconception to avoid:** Do not gloss Easton's "values" as moral values or as material goods. The definition is **things considered valuable, whether they be spiritual or material** — a deliberately wide and neutral category, which is why Lesson 8's list of prevalent values can run from **wealth** and **health** through **skill** and **affection** to **rectitude** and **deference**. Narrowing the term in either direction breaks the universality claim that depends on it.

   -> note: THREE operations, not one. And note PRIORITY in TWO

                              |

   -> cooperation appears here FIRST, before conflict-resolution

   politics "usually involves the ACTIVITIES OF GROUPS of various
