CA found: As reported at Lesson 1.
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

🖼️ **VISUAL — the argument from cornering to withering**

```text
   HOW THE DIVISION ARISES                                  (p.103)
   "under capitalism AND IN EARLIER AGES, production is NOT
    organized on a rational basis. A SMALL MINORITY manages to
    CORNER OWNERSHIP of the major means of production and FORCES
    THE REST OF THE POPULATION TO LIVE ON HARD LABOUR."
   "The emergence of PRIVATE PROPERTY has divided society into
    TWO CLASSES -- the 'haves' and the 'have-nots', the MASTERS
    AND THE SERVANTS, the EXPLOITERS AND THE EXPLOITED -- whose
    interests are IRRECONCILABLE."
   => "Politics and the state are the PRODUCT of this division of
       society -- they continue to SERVE THE INTERESTS OF THE
       DOMINANT CLASS."
                              |
   ENGELS (The Origin of the Family, Private Property and the
   State; 1884), as quoted:
     the state "arose from the need to HOLD CLASS ANTAGONISMS IN
     CHECK"; it is "as a rule, the state of the MOST POWERFUL,
     ECONOMICALLY DOMINANT CLASS, which THROUGH THE MEDIUM OF THE
     STATE, BECOMES ALSO THE POLITICALLY DOMINANT CLASS"
     THREE HISTORICAL CASES, in sequence:
       antiquity : "the state of the SLAVE-OWNERS for the purpose
                    of HOLDING DOWN THE SLAVES"
       feudal    : "the organ of the NOBILITY for holding down the
                    PEASANT SERFS AND BONDSMEN"
       modern    : "an instrument of EXPLOITATION OF THE WAGE
                    LABOUR BY CAPITAL"
                              |
   MARX AND ENGELS (Communist Manifesto; 1848), as quoted:
     "The history of all hitherto existing society is the history
      of CLASS STRUGGLES...  Freeman and slave, patrician and
      plebeian, lord and serf, guild-master and journeyman, in a
      word, OPPRESSOR AND OPPRESSED stood in CONSTANT OPPOSITION
      to one another, carried on an uninterrupted, NOW HIDDEN, NOW
      OPEN fight, a fight that each time ended either in a
      REVOLUTIONARY RECONSTITUTION of society at large, or in the
      COMMON RUIN of the contending classes."
                              |
   WHAT POLITICS THEN DOES
   "This class-conflict is IRRECONCILABLE BY ITS VERY NATURE.
    Politics CANNOT RESOLVE this conflict. ON THE CONTRARY,
    politics is USED BY THE DOMINANT CLASS TO SUPPRESS the
    conflict. This gives THE IMPRESSION OF VOLUNTARY COMPROMISE,
    but in reality it is A COMPROMISE IMPOSED BY THE DOMINANT
    CLASS ON THE DEPENDENT CLASS. It even creates an 'ILLUSION OF
    CONSENT' by administering STRONG IDEOLOGICAL DOSES."
                              |
   AFTER THE REVOLUTION -- "IN REVERSE GEAR"             (p.104)
   capitalism : "exploitation of the WORKERS BY THE CAPITALISTS"
   socialism  : "suppression of the DISPOSSESSED CAPITALISTS BY
                 THE WORKERS (proletariat)" -- in order "to
                 FORESTALL A COUNTERREVOLUTION and to PAVE THE WAY
                 FOR THE BLOSSOMING OF COMMUNISM -- a CLASSLESS
                 SOCIETY"
                              |
   THE END
   "With the elimination of the classes, class conflict will
    disappear and STATE AND POLITICS WOULD NO LONGER BE REQUIRED;
    the state will 'WITHER AWAY'."
   "The END OF CLASS-CONFLICT marks AN END OF POLITICS ITSELF."
```

*Caption: the argument is continuous from a fact about ownership to a prediction about the disappearance of politics. Each step is stated; the chapter's own doubts arrive only at Lesson 22.*

### 1. From cornering to irreconcilability

✅ **A small minority manages to corner ownership of the major means of production and forces the rest of the population to live on hard labour.** ✅ **The emergence of private property has divided society into two classes—the 'haves' and the 'have-nots', the masters and the servants, the exploiters and the exploited—whose interests are irreconcilable.** ✅ **Politics and the state are the product of this division of society—they continue to serve the interests of the dominant class.**

⚠️ **Note the three naming-pairs, which are not synonyms.** ⚠️ **Haves and have-nots** names a distribution. ⚠️ **Masters and servants** names an **authority** relation. ⚠️ **Exploiters and the exploited** names a **transfer**. ⚠️ Using all three is more precise than using one, because the Marxist claim is that a property distribution generates both a command relation and a transfer of value.

⚠️ **And note "and in earlier ages".** ⚠️ The claim is not confined to capitalism; Engels's three cases will confirm the historical reach.

### 2. Engels, and the three cases

> Because the state arose from the need to hold class antagonisms in check ... it is, as a rule, the state of the most powerful, economically dominant class, which through the medium of the state, becomes also the politically dominant class, and thus acquires new means of holding down and exploiting the oppressed class. Thus, the state of antiquity was above all the state of the slave-owners for the purpose of holding down the slaves, as the feudal state was the organ of the nobility for holding down the peasant serfs and bondsmen, and the modern representative state is an instrument of exploitation of the wage labour by capital.
>
> — F. Engels, *The Origin of the Family, Private Property and the State* (1884), as quoted by Gauba, Ch.3, PDF p.103 (the ellipsis is the chapter's)

⚠️ ⚠️ **The mechanism in the middle clause is the valuable part and it is easy to read past.** ⚠️ A class that is **economically dominant** becomes **also the politically dominant class** **through the medium of the state**, and **thus acquires new means of holding down and exploiting**. ⚠️ So the state does not merely **reflect** economic dominance; it **converts** it into political dominance and thereby **adds** instruments the class did not previously have. ⚠️ That is a stronger claim than the base claim of Lesson 16 — the superstructure is not a passive expression but an amplifier.

⚠️ **Note the hedge "as a rule".** ⚠️ Engels does not claim universality without exception. ⚠️ Reproduce it.

⚠️ **The three cases establish historical generality through parallel construction** — slave-owners over slaves, nobility over **peasant serfs and bondsmen**, capital over **wage labour** — each pairing a ruling class with the class it holds down. ⚠️ ❌ No dates are attached to any of the three periods; do not supply them.

### 3. The Manifesto, and the hidden-or-open point

> The history of all hitherto existing society is the history of class struggles... Freeman and slave, patrician and plebeian, lord and serf, guild-master and journeyman, in a word, oppressor and oppressed stood in constant opposition to one another, carried on an uninterrupted, now hidden, now open fight, a fight that each time ended either in a revolutionary reconstitution of society at large, or in the common ruin of the contending classes.
>
> — Marx and Engels, *Communist Manifesto* (1848), as quoted by Gauba, Ch.3, PDF p.103 (the ellipsis is the chapter's)

⚠️ ⚠️ **Two features matter here and both are frequently dropped.**

⚠️ **"Now hidden, now open."** ⚠️ The struggle is **uninterrupted** but not always **visible**. ⚠️ That is the Marxist answer to the obvious objection that many societies appear peaceful: invisibility is not absence. ⚠️ It also pre-licenses the **illusion of consent** at §4 and Lesson 22's **subtle levels** of dominance.

⚠️ ⚠️ **The disjunctive ending: "either in a revolutionary reconstitution of society at large, or in the common ruin of the contending classes."** ⚠️ The second outcome is almost always omitted in exam answers, and omitting it makes the theory look teleological. ⚠️ With it, class struggle has **two** possible terminations and progress is **not** guaranteed. ⚠️ Quoting the full disjunction is a mark of accurate reading.

➡️ ⚠️ **Boundary.** ⚠️ Both quotations are carried by **this** chapter for its own purpose — explaining the nature of politics. ➡️ Topic 04 owns the Marxist system as a doctrine, including these same works. ⚠️ Use them here for the **politics** claim and route the fuller apparatus there.

### 4. Suppression, and the illusion of consent

✅ **This class-conflict is irreconcilable by its very nature. Politics cannot resolve this conflict. On the contrary, politics is used by the dominant class to suppress the conflict. This gives the impression of voluntary compromise, but in reality it is a compromise imposed by the dominant class on the dependent class. It even creates an 'illusion of consent' by administering strong ideological doses.**

⚠️ ⚠️ **This paragraph is the direct answer to Lesson 15's liberal always-claim, and the two should be set side by side in any comparative answer.** ⚠️ The liberal says **some way can always be found** to reconcile; the Marxist says politics **cannot resolve** and instead **suppresses**. ⚠️ Crucially, both are describing the **same observable events** — settlements, agreements, policies. ⚠️ The disagreement is about what those events **are**: genuine reconciliation or **a compromise imposed**.

⚠️ ⚠️ **The illusion of consent is the mechanism that makes the disagreement empirically hard to settle, and saying so is a strong move.** ⚠️ If **strong ideological doses** can produce consent that is only apparent, then the observed acceptance of a settlement cannot by itself establish that the settlement was genuine. ⚠️ ⚠️ And note the connection to the chapter's own framework: Lesson 11 required interest groups to be **conscious of their particular interests**, and the illusion of consent is precisely a mechanism for preventing that condition from being met.

⚠️ **State the residual honestly.** ⚠️ The mechanism is **unfalsifiable as stated** — any observed agreement can be redescribed as imposed, and any contentment as induced. ⚠️ The chapter supplies no criterion for distinguishing genuine from illusory consent. ⚠️ That is a real limitation and conceding it strengthens rather than weakens an answer.

### 5. Reverse gear, and withering away

✅ **This process will continue even after the 'socialist revolution', but in 'reverse gear'.** ✅ While capitalism is **characterized by the exploitation of the workers by the capitalists**, socialism **is characterized by the suppression of the dispossessed capitalists by the workers (proletariat)** — done **in order to forestall a counterrevolution and to pave the way for the blossoming of communism—a classless society**.

⚠️ ⚠️ **The honesty of this passage is notable.** ⚠️ Post-revolutionary socialism is described as **suppression**, in the same vocabulary used for class rule under capitalism. ⚠️ The chapter does not present the transition as the end of domination but as its **inversion**, with a stated purpose — forestalling counterrevolution. ⚠️ ⚠️ That matters for Lesson 22, where the chapter observes that twentieth-century experience **demonstrated that the dream of a 'classless' society is extremely difficult to realize**: a stage defined by suppression is precisely the stage at which a transition can stall.

⚠️ **Note the parenthesis: socialism is "popularly dubbed communism".** ⚠️ The chapter is flagging a loose popular usage while itself distinguishing socialism, as the transitional stage, from **communism—a classless society** as the destination. ⚠️ Reproducing that distinction is worth marks.

✅ **With the elimination of the classes, class conflict will disappear and state and politics would no longer be required; the state will 'wither away'.** ✅ **The end of class-conflict marks an end of politics itself.**

⚠️ ⚠️ **Note that **both** the state and **politics** are said to go.** ⚠️ The withering-away thesis is usually stated about the state alone; here it is extended to politics, which is what makes it a position about the **nature of politics** and therefore this chapter's business. ⚠️ And it is this extension that collides with Easton's **an authoritative allocation of some values is unavoidable** from Lesson 5 — a collision the chapter never stages.

Revision notes:
- ✅ **A small minority manages to corner ownership of the major means of production and forces the rest of the population to live on hard labour**; **private property** divides society into **the 'haves' and the 'have-nots', the masters and the servants, the exploiters and the exploited** — whose interests are **irreconcilable**.
- ✅ **Politics and the state are the product of this division of society—they continue to serve the interests of the dominant class.** ⚠️ Applies **in earlier ages** too.
- ✅ **Engels** (*The Origin of the Family, Private Property and the State*; **1884**): the state **arose from the need to hold class antagonisms in check**; it is **as a rule** the state of the **economically dominant class**, which **through the medium of the state, becomes also the politically dominant class, and thus acquires new means of holding down and exploiting**. Three cases: **slave-owners** / slaves; **nobility** / **peasant serfs and bondsmen**; **the modern representative state** / **wage labour by capital**.
- ⚠️ The state **converts** economic into political dominance and **adds** instruments — stronger than mere reflection. ⚠️ Keep **as a rule**.
- ✅ **Communist Manifesto** (**1848**): **the history of all hitherto existing society is the history of class struggles**; **oppressor and oppressed** in **constant opposition**, an **uninterrupted, now hidden, now open fight** ending **either in a revolutionary reconstitution of society at large, or in the common ruin of the contending classes**.
- ⚠️ **Now hidden, now open** answers the peaceful-society objection. ⚠️ The **common ruin** disjunct must be quoted — progress is not guaranteed.
- ✅ **Politics cannot resolve this conflict … politics is used by the dominant class to suppress the conflict**, giving **the impression of voluntary compromise** though it is **a compromise imposed by the dominant class on the dependent class**, and creating an **'illusion of consent' by administering strong ideological doses**.
- ⚠️ Liberal and Marxist accounts describe the **same events** and disagree about what they **are**. ⚠️ The illusion mechanism is unfalsifiable as stated — concede this.
- ✅ After the revolution the process continues **in 'reverse gear'**: **suppression of the dispossessed capitalists by the workers (proletariat)**, to **forestall a counterrevolution** and **pave the way for the blossoming of communism—a classless society**.
- ✅ **State and politics would no longer be required; the state will 'wither away'**; **the end of class-conflict marks an end of politics itself.**
- ⚠️ **Both** state and politics go — which is what collides with Easton's **unavoidable** allocation.

### Concept check

**Question:** The liberal and Marxist views describe the same settlements. What exactly do they disagree about, and can the disagreement be settled by observation?

**Model answer:** They disagree about the **nature** of what is observed, not about what is observed, and on the Marxist account the disagreement cannot be settled by observation alone. ⚠️ Both acknowledge that groups reach agreements. ⚠️ For the liberal these are genuine reconciliations: **some way can always be found to ensure the reconciliation of their conflicting interests**, and the parties, having become aware of the **common interest**, are **prepared to accept the solution**. ⚠️ For the Marxist the identical event is **a compromise imposed by the dominant class on the dependent class** which merely **gives the impression of voluntary compromise**, since **politics is used by the dominant class to suppress the conflict**. ⚠️ The decisive move is the next sentence: it **even creates an 'illusion of consent' by administering strong ideological doses**. ⚠️ That blocks the obvious test, because the evidence a liberal would cite — that the parties accepted the settlement and appear content — is exactly what the Marxist explains as manufactured. ⚠️ Within the chapter's own framework the mechanism is well-targeted, since Lesson 11 made consciousness of interest a condition of a political situation, and the illusion of consent is a device for preventing that condition from being met. ⚠️ The residual must be conceded: as stated, the thesis is unfalsifiable, because no observable agreement could count against it and the chapter supplies no criterion for telling genuine from illusory consent. ⚠️ The chapter's own conclusion effectively acknowledges the difficulty by shifting ground, holding that **conditions of 'dominance' and 'dependence' exist in society at many subtle levels** and that justice is achieved **when these conditions are closely scrutinized and straightened** — an invitation to examine the conditions rather than to read off the verdict from the settlement.

**Misconception to avoid:** Do not quote the Manifesto passage without its second disjunct. The struggle ends **either in a revolutionary reconstitution of society at large, or in the common ruin of the contending classes**, so the theory allows a second and catastrophic outcome. Omitting it converts a conditional historical claim into a guarantee of progress, which the text does not state and the chapter's own later doubts about realising a classless society do not support.

## Lesson 18 — Communitarian view I: cooperation, not conflict

Progress: 18 / 24 | Stage: Advanced | Subtopic: The claim about human nature, and Aristotle's organ-and-organism analogy

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Canonical Core owner §§6 and 8 with the Advanced companion's §§5 and 6, read against the source chapter's section headed "The Communitarian View", subsection "Politics is the Arena of Mutual Cooperation, Not of Conflict" (Gauba, Ch.3, PDF p.104), which supplies the human-nature claim, the mutual-aid foundation, Aristotle's analogy, the good-life claim and the continuity with modern communitarians.
CA search: Not re-run for this lesson; see Lesson 1.
CA found: As reported there.
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

🖼️ **VISUAL — a claim about nature, and the analogy that supports it**

```text
   THE FOUNDATIONAL CLAIM                                   (p.104)
   "According to the communitarian view, the ESSENCE OF HUMAN
    NATURE lies in the SPIRIT OF COOPERATION, NOT OF CONFLICT."
   "Hence MUTUAL AID OR COOPERATION is the FOUNDATION of
    political organization."
                              |
   NOTE WHAT THIS DISPLACES:
     Liberal : conflict exists, "not very deep"  -> manage it
     Marxist : conflict is "irreconcilable"      -> end its cause
     Communitarian: conflict is NOT THE STARTING POINT AT ALL
                              |
   THE ARISTOTELIAN SUPPORT
   "Aristotle had argued that the relation between INDIVIDUAL AND
    THE STATE was SIMILAR TO THAT BETWEEN AN ORGAN AND THE
    ORGANISM."
                              |
        ORGAN : ORGANISM  ::  INDIVIDUAL : STATE
        |
        what the analogy carries:
          - the organ has NO FUNCTION apart from the organism
          - its IDENTITY is given by its place in the whole
          - the whole is not the SUM of the organs
          - separation is not independence but DEATH
                              |
   WHAT ARISTOTLE CONCLUDED, per the chapter
   "The state was AN INDISPENSABLE INSTRUMENT OF GOOD LIFE FOR
    ALL HUMAN BEINGS."
   "Hence different individuals MUST COOPERATE WITH EACH OTHER in
    order to secure GOOD LIFE FOR EVERYONE."
                              |
   THE CONTINUITY CLAIM
   "MODERN COMMUNITARIANS POSTULATE A SIMILAR ROLE of the
    individual in a larger social and political organization."
```

*Caption: the communitarian view begins where the other two end. It does not propose a better way of handling conflict; it denies that conflict is the fundamental fact about social life.*

### 1. The claim about human nature

✅ **According to the communitarian view, the essence of human nature lies in the spirit of cooperation, not of conflict. Hence mutual aid or cooperation is the foundation of political organization.**

⚠️ ⚠️ **This is a claim about **nature**, and that is what makes it radical rather than merely optimistic.** ⚠️ It does not say cooperation is desirable, or achievable, or more efficient. ⚠️ It says cooperation is what human nature **essentially is**, with conflict as the departure from it. ⚠️ Everything the view says afterwards follows from that premise: if cooperation is the essence, then observed conflict must be explained as a **deviation** — which is exactly what Lesson 13 recorded, that **the impression of conflict arises from misunderstanding of our real interest**.

⚠️ **The structural consequence for comparison is large and should be stated early in any answer.** ⚠️ The liberal and Marxist views both take **conflict** as the datum and differ about its depth and curability. ⚠️ The communitarian view changes the datum to **cooperation**. ⚠️ That is why Lesson 13's chapter-word was **redefines**: a theory with a different starting fact is not a third answer but a different question.

⚠️ ⚠️ **And note the second sentence's consequence for Lesson 6.** ⚠️ Roberts's definition held that the political process **involves both cooperation and the resolution of conflict** — and the chapter labelled that **a liberal view**. ⚠️ Here cooperation is not one component among two but **the foundation** of political organization. ⚠️ Setting the two formulations side by side shows precisely how far the communitarian claim goes beyond merely valuing cooperation.

⚠️ ❌ **State the evidential limit.** ❌ No argument, evidence or authority is offered for the human-nature claim on this page; it is asserted as the view's starting point. ⚠️ Cite it as the doctrine's **premise**, and note that Lesson 22's conclusion effectively limits it by confining the view's applicability to societies **whose members are largely satisfied with their status**.

### 2. Aristotle's analogy

✅ **Aristotle had argued that the relation between individual and the state was similar to that between an organ and the organism. The state was an indispensable instrument of good life for all human beings. Hence different individuals must cooperate with each other in order to secure good life for everyone.**

⚠️ ⚠️ **The analogy is the view's main argumentative resource and it repays unpacking, since the chapter states it without drawing out what it carries.**

| Feature of the organ-organism relation | ⚠️ What it implies for individual and state |
|---|---|
| An organ has no function apart from the organism | The individual's purposes are intelligible only within the political whole |
| The organ's identity is given by its place in the whole | ⚠️ Anticipates Lesson 19's claim that identity develops **only from his place in the community** |
| The whole is not the sum of its organs | ⚠️ The common good is not an aggregate — which is exactly the liberal **equilibrium** it rejects |
| Separation is not independence but death | ⚠️ The strongest and most dangerous implication, and the chapter does **not** draw it |

⚠️ ⚠️ **That last row is where care is required, and an answer should say so.** ⚠️ Pressed hard, an organic analogy can license subordinating the individual entirely to the whole — the move Topic 05 recorded fascism making when it treated the state as **an end in itself**. ⚠️ ❌ The chapter does **not** take that step: it says the state is **an indispensable instrument of good life for all human beings**, which makes the state **instrumental** and the good of **all human beings** the end. ⚠️ ⚠️ That word **instrument** is the chapter's own safeguard, and reproducing it is what separates an accurate account of communitarianism from the collectivist caricature.

⚠️ **Note the universal quantifier, twice.** ⚠️ **Good life for all human beings**; **good life for everyone**. ⚠️ The end is distributed across every member, not aggregated over the whole. ⚠️ That blocks the reading on which some may be sacrificed for the collective.

⚠️ ❌ **Boundary.** ❌ The chapter supplies no Aristotelian apparatus beyond this analogy — no *polis*, no *telos*, no account of virtue, no work cited and no dates. ⚠️ Lesson 1 noted only that Aristotle used *Politics* as a title. ➡️ Aristotle's political theory belongs to its own owners. ❌ Do not reconstruct it here.

### 3. The continuity claim

✅ **Modern communitarians postulate a similar role of the individual in a larger social and political organization.**

⚠️ **One sentence doing a lot of work, and its hedge should be kept.** ⚠️ **A similar role** — not the same doctrine. ⚠️ The chapter is claiming a continuity of **structure** between Aristotle and the contemporary exponents of Lesson 20, not that MacIntyre, Taylor and Sandel are Aristotelians.

⚠️ 🔶 **The owner sharpens this.** 🔶 The basic owner insists that the three contemporary communitarians must **not** be treated as **one undifferentiated voice**, since each **targets a different weakness in liberalism**. ⚠️ So the continuity is real at the level of the organ-organism intuition and breaks down at the level of mechanism — which is Lesson 20's content and Lesson 23's discipline.

Revision notes:
- ✅ **The essence of human nature lies in the spirit of cooperation, not of conflict.** ✅ **Mutual aid or cooperation is the foundation of political organization.**
- ⚠️ ⚠️ A claim about **nature**, not about desirability. Conflict becomes the **deviation** to be explained — hence **the impression of conflict arises from misunderstanding of our real interest**.
- ⚠️ Liberal and Marxist views take conflict as the datum; this view changes the datum. That is why the chapter says it **redefines** politics.
- ⚠️ Contrast with Lesson 6: Roberts had cooperation as **one of two** components and the chapter called that **a liberal view**; here cooperation is **the foundation**.
- ❌ No evidence or argument is offered for the human-nature claim — it is the view's premise.
- ✅ **Aristotle had argued that the relation between individual and the state was similar to that between an organ and the organism.**
- ✅ **The state was an indispensable instrument of good life for all human beings**; **different individuals must cooperate with each other in order to secure good life for everyone**.
- ⚠️ ⚠️ **Instrument** is the chapter's safeguard against the collectivist reading; **all human beings** and **everyone** distribute the end across members. ❌ The chapter does **not** draw the separation-is-death implication.
- ❌ No Aristotelian apparatus — no *polis*, *telos*, virtue theory, work or dates. ➡️ Route to Aristotle's owners.
- ✅ **Modern communitarians postulate a similar role of the individual in a larger social and political organization** — ⚠️ **similar**, not identical; 🔶 the owner forbids treating the three as one voice.

### Concept check

**Question:** What stops the organ-and-organism analogy from becoming a collectivist doctrine in which individuals may be sacrificed to the whole?

**Model answer:** Two features of the chapter's own formulation, and both must be quoted because the analogy on its own would not stop it. ⚠️ Pressed to its limit, an organic analogy supports exactly that conclusion: an organ exists for the organism, has no independent purpose, and may be sacrificed if the body requires it — which is structurally the move Topic 05 recorded fascism making in treating the state as **an end in itself** with the individual as **the means**. ⚠️ The chapter blocks it first by making the state **instrumental**: it is **an indispensable instrument of good life for all human beings**, so the state is a means and human good is the end, which inverts the fascist relation rather than approaching it. ⚠️ It blocks it second by **distributing** the end across every member, twice over — **good life for all human beings** and **good life for everyone** — so there is no aggregate whole whose good could be served by subtracting from some member's. ⚠️ Lesson 19 adds a third safeguard, since the individual **requires a place in a well-functioning community** **in order to flourish in life**, which makes the community's function the flourishing of its members. ⚠️ The honest residual is that the chapter supplies these safeguards by **formulation** rather than by argument: it does not explain why the analogy should be stopped at the instrumental reading, and the advanced owner flags the standing risk that **if fraternity is detached from liberty and equality, communitarian language can slide into conformity**. ⚠️ A strong answer therefore states both the safeguard and the fragility, rather than presenting communitarianism as either obviously benign or obviously collectivist.

**Misconception to avoid:** Do not reconstruct Aristotle's political philosophy from this page. The chapter supplies one analogy and one conclusion — the organ-and-organism comparison and the claim that the state is **an indispensable instrument of good life** — with no *polis* theory, no account of *telos* or virtue, no work cited and no dates. Everything beyond those two sentences must come from Aristotle's own owners and be attributed there.

## Lesson 19 — Communitarian view II: community, identity, two common goods

Progress: 19 / 24 | Stage: Advanced | Subtopic: The definition of community, the two directions of the good, and the strongest sentence in the doctrine

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Canonical Core owner §§3, 5 and 6 with the Advanced companion's §§4 and 6, read against the source chapter's paragraphs on community and identity and its subsection "Politics is Instrumental to the Pursuit of the Common Good" (Gauba, Ch.3, PDF pp.104-105), which supply the identity claim, the definition of community, the define-versus-discover contrast, the two notions of common good and the indebtedness argument.

CA found: As reported there.
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

🖼️ **VISUAL — the identity claim, and the two common goods set against each other**

```text
   THE IDENTITY CLAIM                                       (p.104)
   "an individual develops his IDENTITY, TALENTS and PURSUITS IN
    LIFE ONLY FROM HIS PLACE IN THE COMMUNITY"
                              |
   WHAT A COMMUNITY IS -- the chapter's definition
   "a set of SOCIAL RELATIONSHIPS based on sharing
        COMMON CHARACTERISTICS | COMMON VALUES | COMMON INTERESTS"
   "In order to FLOURISH IN LIFE an individual REQUIRES A PLACE IN
    A WELL-FUNCTIONING COMMUNITY."
                              |
   THE DECISIVE CONTRAST -- two verbs
   +------------------------------+------------------------------+
   | LIBERALS                     | COMMUNITARIANS               |
   | "encourage each person to    | "direct him to DISCOVER AND  |
   |  DEFINE AND SEEK HIS OWN     |  PURSUE his 'good' AS AN     |
   |  'GOOD' WITHIN the political |  INTEGRAL PART OF THE 'GOOD  |
   |  structure"                  |  OF THE COMMUNITY'"          |
   |                              |                              |
   | DEFINE = the good is MADE by | DISCOVER = the good is FOUND |
   |          the chooser         |            already there     |
   +------------------------------+------------------------------+
   "This can be accomplished ONLY THROUGH COOPERATION, NOT THROUGH
    COMPETITION."
                              |
   THE TWO NOTIONS OF COMMON GOOD                      (pp.104-105)
   "The idea of common good is THE KEYNOTE of communitarianism.
    Its notion of common good is DIFFERENT FROM THAT OF
    LIBERALISM."
   LIBERAL : "if EACH INDIVIDUAL is allowed to pursue his
              SELF-INTEREST, common interest would be served
              AUTOMATICALLY AS A CONSEQUENCE"
   COMMUNITARIAN : "does NOT BELIEVE that ISOLATED INDIVIDUALS
              COULD HAVE DIFFERENT INTERESTS. It does NOT
              RECOGNIZE EVEN THEIR RIGHT to pursue self-interest
              APART FROM the interest of the community itself."
                              |
   THE INDEBTEDNESS ARGUMENT                                (p.105)
   LIBERALISM : "an individual is NOT INDEBTED to society for his
                 existence and his potentialities; hence he is
                 ABSOLUTELY FREE to pursue his SELF-APPOINTED
                 GOALS"
   COMMUNITARIANISM : "the individual OWES his existence and his
                 potentialities TO SOCIETY; hence he has NO RIGHT
                 to apply these potentialities in the so-called
                 self-interest, DISREGARDING THE INTEREST OF
                 SOCIETY"
   => "His COMMITMENT TO SOCIETY is AN ESSENTIAL FEATURE OF HIS
       PERSONALITY."
```

*Caption: the define/discover pair is the sharpest two-word statement of the liberal-communitarian difference in the whole chapter, and the indebtedness argument is what licenses it.*

### 1. Identity and community

✅ **Communitarians tend to argue that an individual develops his identity, talents and pursuits in life only from his place in the community.** ✅ Community is **a set of social relationships based on sharing common characteristics, common values and common interests**. ✅ **In order to flourish in life an individual requires a place in a well-functioning community.**

⚠️ **Three things are said to come from community, and they escalate.** ⚠️ **Talents** is the weakest — plainly developed socially. ⚠️ **Pursuits** is stronger, since what one aims at is shaped by what is available and valued. ⚠️ **Identity** is strongest: it says **who one is** is constituted socially. ⚠️ The **only** before **from his place in the community** is what makes these constitutive rather than merely causal claims.

⚠️ **The definition of community has three components and they are not redundant.** ⚠️ **Common characteristics** is given — language, descent, locality. ⚠️ **Common values** is evaluative. ⚠️ **Common interests** is practical. ⚠️ A group sharing only characteristics is a category, not a community on this definition.

⚠️ ⚠️ **"Well-functioning" is a quiet but crucial qualifier.** ⚠️ The claim is not that any community suffices but that flourishing requires **a well-functioning** one. ⚠️ That concedes that communities can function badly — and it is the single textual foothold within the chapter for the objections the owners raise at 🔶 Lesson 23 about conformism, exclusion and oppressive tradition. ⚠️ ❌ The chapter itself develops none of them.

### 2. Define against discover

✅ **Whereas liberals encourage each person to define and seek his own 'good' within the political structure, communitarians direct him to discover and pursue his 'good' as an integral part of the 'good of the community'. This can be accomplished only through cooperation, not through competition.**

⚠️ ⚠️ **The verbs carry the whole difference and should be quoted as a pair.** ⚠️ To **define** a good is to **make** it — it is one's own because one chose it. ⚠️ To **discover** a good is to **find** it — it was there before the finding, and getting it right or wrong is possible. ⚠️ That is a difference about the **status** of the good, not merely about its content.

⚠️ **Two consequences follow, and both are examinable.** ⚠️ First, if the good is discovered, one can be **mistaken** about one's own good — which is exactly the claim at Lesson 13 that conflict arises from **misunderstanding of our real interest**. ⚠️ A view on which goods are defined cannot say this, since there is nothing to be mistaken about. ⚠️ Second, discovery is naturally **cooperative** — several people investigating one object can help each other — whereas definition is naturally **competitive**, since separately chosen goods may be incompatible. ⚠️ That is why the chapter can conclude **only through cooperation, not through competition** rather than merely asserting a preference.

⚠️ **Note also "within the political structure" on the liberal side.** ⚠️ Even the liberal is not an atomist about **constraints** — the good is pursued **within** a structure. ⚠️ The difference is that for the liberal the structure is a **framework** for separately chosen goods, while for the communitarian the community supplies the **content** of the good.

### 3. The two notions of the common good

✅ **The idea of common good is the keynote of communitarianism. Its notion of common good is different from that of liberalism.** ✅ Liberalism holds that **if each individual is allowed to pursue his self-interest, common interest would be served automatically as a consequence.** ✅ Communitarianism **does not believe that isolated individuals could have different interests. It does not recognize even their right to pursue self-interest apart from the interest of the community itself.**

⚠️ ⚠️ **The liberal formulation here is stronger than the one at Lesson 15, and the difference is worth noticing.** ⚠️ Lesson 15 had the common good emerging through **awareness** and **interaction** — a deliberative process. ⚠️ Here it is served **automatically as a consequence** of each pursuing self-interest — an invisible-hand formulation. ⚠️ The chapter gives both without reconciling them, and an answer can legitimately note that the communitarian critique is aimed at the **stronger, automatic** version.

⚠️ ⚠️ **The communitarian sentence is the most demanding in the doctrine and must be handled with precision.** ⚠️ Two claims are made, and they are different in kind.

| Claim | Kind | ⚠️ Careful reading |
|---|---|---|
| **Does not believe that isolated individuals could have different interests** | **Metaphysical** | ⚠️ About **isolated** individuals — persons abstracted from community. The claim is that so abstracted there is no determinate interest to have, not that actual people never differ |
| **Does not recognize even their right to pursue self-interest apart from the interest of the community** | **Normative** | ⚠️ ⚠️ The strongest sentence in the chapter's communitarianism. Note: **apart from** the community's interest — not self-interest as such |

⚠️ ⚠️ **The phrase "apart from" is load-bearing and dropping it produces the caricature.** ⚠️ What is denied is a right to pursue self-interest **in abstraction from or in disregard of** the community's interest. ⚠️ The following page confirms the reading: the individual has **no right to apply these potentialities in the so-called self-interest, disregarding the interest of society**. ⚠️ **Disregarding** is the operative word. ❌ The chapter does not say that self-interest is forbidden, or that individuals have no rights, or that the community may override every personal pursuit.

⚠️ ⚠️ **But do not soften it too far either.** ⚠️ This remains a genuinely demanding position: it withholds a **right** that liberalism grants, and the word **even** signals that the chapter knows it is stating something strong. ⚠️ The honest presentation is that communitarianism conditions the exercise of self-interest on its compatibility with the community's interest — which is more than an exhortation and less than a prohibition. ⚠️ 🔶 This is exactly where Lesson 23's objection-reply chains operate.

### 4. The indebtedness argument

✅ **Liberalism holds that an individual is not indebted to society for his existence and his potentialities; hence he is absolutely free to pursue his self-appointed goals. In contrast, communitarianism believes that the individual owes his existence and his potentialities to society; hence he has no right to apply these potentialities in the so-called self-interest, disregarding the interest of society. His commitment to society is an essential feature of his personality.**

⚠️ ⚠️ **This is the chapter's clearest statement of **why** the normative claim follows, and it is an argument rather than an assertion.** ⚠️ The structure is: a factual premise about **origin** (**owes his existence and his potentialities to society**) yields a normative conclusion about **entitlement** (**no right to apply these potentialities … disregarding the interest of society**). ⚠️ Capacities that were socially produced cannot be deployed as though they were wholly one's own.

⚠️ **Note that the chapter presents the liberal side as the mirror image**, with the same structure and the opposite premise: **not indebted** therefore **absolutely free**. ⚠️ Setting the two out in parallel is the most efficient way to examine the dispute, because it localises the disagreement in a single factual claim about indebtedness.

⚠️ ⚠️ **The closing sentence is the deepest one: "his commitment to society is an essential feature of his personality."** ⚠️ **Essential** means constitutive — the commitment is not an obligation added to a person already complete but part of what makes them the person they are. ⚠️ 🔶 That is precisely the claim Sandel will press against the **unencumbered self** at Lesson 20 and 🔶 Lesson 23, and it is the point at which the doctrine's metaphysics and its politics meet.

Revision notes:
- ✅ **An individual develops his identity, talents and pursuits in life only from his place in the community** — ⚠️ **only** makes these constitutive claims.
- ✅ **Community represents a set of social relationships based on sharing common characteristics, common values and common interests.** ✅ **In order to flourish in life an individual requires a place in a well-functioning community.**
- ⚠️ **Well-functioning** concedes that communities can function badly — the chapter's only internal foothold for the 🔶 conformism and exclusion objections, which it does not develop.
- ✅ **Whereas liberals encourage each person to define and seek his own 'good' within the political structure, communitarians direct him to discover and pursue his 'good' as an integral part of the 'good of the community'.** ✅ **This can be accomplished only through cooperation, not through competition.**
- ⚠️ ⚠️ **Define** = made; **discover** = found. Hence one can be **mistaken** about one's own good, and discovery is naturally cooperative where definition is naturally competitive.
- ✅ **The idea of common good is the keynote of communitarianism.** Liberal version: **if each individual is allowed to pursue his self-interest, common interest would be served automatically as a consequence** — ⚠️ a stronger, invisible-hand form than Lesson 15's deliberative one.
- ✅ Communitarianism **does not believe that isolated individuals could have different interests** and **does not recognize even their right to pursue self-interest apart from the interest of the community itself**.
- ⚠️ ⚠️ **Isolated** and **apart from** are load-bearing. ❌ Not a denial of self-interest or of rights as such; ⚠️ but genuinely demanding — a **right** is withheld.
- ✅ Indebtedness: liberalism says **not indebted … hence absolutely free**; communitarianism says the individual **owes his existence and his potentialities to society; hence he has no right to apply these potentialities in the so-called self-interest, disregarding the interest of society**.
- ✅ **His commitment to society is an essential feature of his personality** — ⚠️ **essential** = constitutive; the bridge to Sandel.

### Concept check

**Question:** Communitarianism "does not recognize even their right to pursue self-interest apart from the interest of the community itself." State what this does and does not claim.

**Model answer:** It claims that the pursuit of self-interest is **conditioned** on its compatibility with the community's interest; it does not claim that self-interest is forbidden or that individuals have no rights. ⚠️ The decisive words are **apart from**, and the following page confirms the reading by saying the individual has **no right to apply these potentialities in the so-called self-interest, disregarding the interest of society** — where **disregarding** is the operative term. ⚠️ What is withheld is a right to pursue one's good **in abstraction from or in defiance of** the community's good, which is compatible with pursuing one's good **as an integral part of** it, exactly as the preceding sentence prescribes. ⚠️ The ground for the restriction is the indebtedness argument, which is an argument rather than an assertion: because the individual **owes his existence and his potentialities to society**, capacities that were socially produced may not be deployed as though wholly one's own — and the chapter states the liberal mirror-image, that one who is **not indebted** is correspondingly **absolutely free**. ⚠️ Three things are therefore **not** claimed: that individuals lack rights, which the chapter nowhere says; that self-interest is illegitimate, since the complaint is against the **so-called** self-interest pursued in disregard of society; and that dissent may be suppressed, which the advanced owner expressly forbids reading into the duty emphasis. ⚠️ But the position must not be softened into a platitude either: the word **even** signals that a right liberalism grants is being withheld, and the metaphysical companion claim — that it **does not believe that isolated individuals could have different interests** — is a real denial about persons **abstracted from** community, not merely a reminder that people are sociable. ⚠️ The accurate summary is that communitarianism conditions rather than prohibits, which is more demanding than exhortation and less than coercion.

**Misconception to avoid:** Do not treat the liberal account of the common good as single and settled in this chapter. Lesson 15 presented it deliberatively — **awareness of the conflicting interests … and their interaction leads to an awareness of the 'common interest'** — while this page presents it automatically, as something served **automatically as a consequence** of each pursuing self-interest. The chapter gives both without reconciling them, and the communitarian critique is aimed at the stronger automatic version.

## Lesson 20 — Communitarian view III: MacIntyre, Taylor, Sandel

🖼️ **VISUAL — three critics, three targets**

```text
   ALASDAIR MACINTYRE (After Virtue; 1981)                  (p.105)
   POSITIVE CLAIM
   "individuals DEVELOP AND PERFECT VIRTUE THROUGH COOPERATIVE
    HUMAN ACTIVITY. It is designed to achieve such STANDARDS OF
    EXCELLENCE which human beings are capable to achieve."
   NEGATIVE CLAIM -- the warning
   "IF the state allows 'SOCIALLY DISCONNECTED' individuals to
    pursue their so-called 'SELF-APPOINTED GOALS', the result
    would be SOCIAL DISINTEGRATION AND MORAL DISASTER."
   THE EVIDENCE OFFERED
   "In fact the process of such disintegration HAS ALREADY STARTED
    IN SOME MODERN LIBERAL STATES as evident in:"
       CRIME AND VIOLENCE | THE BREAKDOWN OF THE FAMILY |
       THE WIDESPREAD DRUG ABUSE
                              |
   CHARLES TAYLOR (Philosophical Papers; 1985)
   "attacks the liberals' 'ATOMISTIC' CONCEPTION of human beings
    WHICH IGNORES THE FACT that a human being MUST BE 'SITUATED'
    IN A SOCIETY in order to DEVELOP HIS PERSONALITY"
   -> target: the ATOMISTIC conception
                              |
   MICHAEL SANDEL (Liberalism and the Limits of Justice; 1982)
   "criticizes the liberals' conception of DISJOINTED NATURE of
    the people"
   "views human nature as 'EMBEDDED' in a PARTICULAR TIME, PLACE
    AND CULTURE"
   "insists on creating a 'DEEPER COMMONALITY' informed by
    'SHARED SELF-UNDERSTANDING' AS WELL AS AFFECTION"
   -> target: the DISJOINTED conception; remedy: DEEPER COMMONALITY
                              |
   THE CHAPTER'S CLOSING SUMMARY
   "In a nutshell, the communitarian view treats politics as AN
    ACTIVITY CONCERNED WITH THE IDENTIFICATION AND PURSUIT OF
    THEIR COMMON INTEREST."
   -> note BOTH verbs: IDENTIFICATION and PURSUIT
```

*Caption: the chapter gives each thinker one target and one sentence. 🔶 The owner insists they are not interchangeable — which is why Lesson 23 supplies the differentiated mechanisms separately.*

### 1. MacIntyre: virtue through cooperative activity

✅ **Alasdair MacIntyre (After Virtue; 1981) argues that the individuals develop and perfect virtue through cooperative human activity. It is designed to achieve such standards of excellence which human beings are capable to achieve.**

⚠️ **Two elements, and the second is the distinctive one.** ⚠️ Virtue comes **through cooperative human activity** — so it is not a private achievement. ⚠️ And that activity is **designed to achieve such standards of excellence which human beings are capable to achieve** — so it has **internal standards**, independent of what any participant happens to want.

⚠️ ⚠️ **That second element is what makes this an argument rather than a preference.** ⚠️ If an activity has standards of excellence proper to it, then doing it well is not a matter of individual choice, and learning to do it well requires initiation by others who already meet the standard. ⚠️ Cooperation is therefore not merely helpful but **constitutive** of acquiring virtue. 🔶 The owner's §13 names the underlying machinery — practices and their internal goods — and that is carried at Lesson 23 rather than attributed to this page.

### 2. MacIntyre: the warning, and its evidence

✅ **If the state allows 'socially disconnected' individuals to pursue their so-called 'self-appointed goals', the result would be social disintegration and moral disaster.** ✅ **In fact the process of such disintegration has already started in some modern liberal states as evident in the prevalence of crime and violence, the breakdown of the family, and the widespread drug abuse.**

⚠️ ⚠️ **Handle this passage with real care, for three separate reasons.**

1. ⚠️ **The vocabulary is marked.** ⚠️ **Socially disconnected** and **self-appointed goals** both appear in the chapter's quotation marks, and **so-called** before **self-appointed goals** signals that the chapter regards the description as the liberal's own rather than an accurate one. ⚠️ **Self-appointed goals** is also the exact phrase the p.107 **Liberty** box uses to define liberty — **fulfilling his self-appointed goals** — so the critique is aimed at the liberal principle in its own words.
2. ⚠️ ⚠️ **The empirical claim is unsupported.** ❌ No study, country, period, data or source is given for **crime and violence**, **the breakdown of the family** or **the widespread drug abuse**. ⚠️ **Some modern liberal states** names nobody. ⚠️ It is a **2009** assertion. ❌ Do not reproduce it as evidence of anything; cite it as the form the communitarian warning takes.
3. ⚠️ ⚠️ **The causal inference is the weakest link and an answer should say so.** ⚠️ Even granting the three phenomena, attributing them to the toleration of **socially disconnected** individuals pursuing **self-appointed goals** is a causal claim with no argument behind it, and plainly competing explanations exist. ⚠️ ⚠️ Note moreover that the chapter supplies a rival explanation for very similar phenomena **elsewhere in the same book**: Lesson 13's own diagnosis attributed modern unhappiness to a want of **emotional security** rather than to state permissiveness. ⚠️ Naming the gap between the diagnosis and the causal attribution is a legitimate internal criticism.

### 3. Taylor and Sandel

✅ **Charles Taylor (Philosophical Papers; 1985) attacks the liberals' 'atomistic' conception of human beings which ignores the fact that a human being must be 'situated' in a society in order to develop his personality.**

⚠️ **One target, named: the **atomistic** conception.** ⚠️ The claim is about a **precondition** — being **situated** is required **in order to develop his personality**. ⚠️ Note that the charge is that atomism **ignores a fact**, which makes it an accusation of descriptive error rather than of moral failing.

✅ **Michael Sandel (Liberalism and the Limits of Justice; 1982) criticizes the liberals' conception of disjointed nature of the people. Sandel views human nature as 'embedded' in a particular time, place and culture. He insists on creating a 'deeper commonality' informed by 'shared self-understanding' as well as affection.**

⚠️ **Three moves, and Sandel is the only one of the three given a positive programme.** ⚠️ The target is the **disjointed** conception; the alternative is nature **embedded in a particular time, place and culture**; and the prescription is a **deeper commonality** resting on **shared self-understanding** **as well as affection**.

⚠️ ⚠️ **"As well as affection" is the under-noticed phrase and it matters.** ⚠️ Shared understanding alone would be a cognitive bond; adding **affection** makes the required commonality **affective** too. ⚠️ That links directly to Lesson 13's diagnosis of a want of **emotional security** and to the p.107 table's description of communitarian society as a set of **Intellectually and Emotionally United Human Beings** — the two adverbs matching Sandel's two components exactly.

⚠️ ❌ **Boundary, stated firmly.** ⚠️ The chapter gives each thinker **one sentence**. ❌ Nothing about Rawls, the original position, the veil of ignorance or the **unencumbered self** appears on this page, and none of it may be attributed to the chapter. 🔶 That material comes from the owner's §13 and is carried at Lesson 23 under its own marker. ⚠️ Note also that the chapter cites Taylor's *Philosophical Papers* (1985), whereas 🔶 the owner cites *Sources of the Self* (1989) and "The Politics of Recognition" (1994) — different works for different purposes, and the distinction is recorded in the Source Ledger.

⚠️ ❌ **Scan-artefact disclosure.** The extraction prints **Maclntyre** and renders Sandel's title as **Liberalism and the Limits of justice** with a lowercase **j**; both are normalised and catalogued.

### 4. The closing summary

✅ **In a nutshell, the communitarian view treats politics as an activity concerned with the identification and pursuit of their common interest.**

⚠️ ⚠️ **Both nouns matter and most answers quote only the second.** ⚠️ **Identification** is the epistemic task — finding out what the common interest is, which is possible only because, on this view, it exists prior to the finding. ⚠️ **Pursuit** is the practical task. ⚠️ A view that held the common good to be **made** rather than **found** would have no use for the first verb, so **identification** is precisely where the communitarian account differs from the liberal one, exactly as Lesson 19's define/discover pair predicted.

Revision notes:
- ✅ **Alasdair MacIntyre** (*After Virtue*; **1981**): individuals **develop and perfect virtue through cooperative human activity**, which is **designed to achieve such standards of excellence which human beings are capable to achieve**.
- ⚠️ Internal standards make cooperation **constitutive** of acquiring virtue, not merely helpful. 🔶 Practices and internal goods are the owner's addition — Lesson 23.
- ✅ The warning: if the state allows **'socially disconnected'** individuals to pursue **so-called 'self-appointed goals'**, the result is **social disintegration and moral disaster**; disintegration **has already started in some modern liberal states** as evident in **crime and violence, the breakdown of the family, and the widespread drug abuse**.
- ❌ Unsupported — no study, country, period or data; **2009**. ❌ Never cite as evidence. ⚠️ The causal attribution is the weakest link, and the chapter's own Lesson 13 diagnosis offers a rival explanation.
- ⚠️ **Self-appointed goals** is the same phrase the p.107 **Liberty** box uses — the critique targets the liberal principle in its own words.
- ✅ **Charles Taylor** (*Philosophical Papers*; **1985**) attacks the **'atomistic' conception** which **ignores the fact that a human being must be 'situated' in a society in order to develop his personality**.
- ✅ **Michael Sandel** (*Liberalism and the Limits of Justice*; **1982**) criticizes the **disjointed nature** conception; views human nature as **'embedded' in a particular time, place and culture**; insists on a **'deeper commonality' informed by 'shared self-understanding' as well as affection**.
- ⚠️ **As well as affection** matches the table's **Intellectually and Emotionally United Human Beings**.
- ❌ No Rawls, original position, veil of ignorance or **unencumbered self** on this page. 🔶 Owner material — Lesson 23. ⚠️ The owner cites different Taylor works from the chapter.
- ✅ The communitarian view treats politics as **an activity concerned with the identification and pursuit of their common interest** — ⚠️ **both** verbs.

### Concept check

**Question:** How should MacIntyre's claim that social disintegration "has already started in some modern liberal states" be used in an answer, and how should it not?

**Model answer:** It should be used as a statement of the **form** the communitarian warning takes, and never as evidence that the warning is correct. ⚠️ The claim as the chapter gives it is that if the state allows **'socially disconnected'** individuals to pursue **so-called 'self-appointed goals'**, the result is **social disintegration and moral disaster**, and that the process **has already started in some modern liberal states** as shown by **crime and violence, the breakdown of the family, and the widespread drug abuse**. ⚠️ Three defects bar its evidential use. ❌ No state is named, so **some modern liberal states** is unverifiable as stated. ❌ No study, data, period or source is supplied for any of the three phenomena. ⚠️ And the assertion belongs to a **2009** edition, so even its implicit present tense is dated. ⚠️ The deeper problem is the **causal inference**: granting all three phenomena, attributing them to state toleration of disconnected individuals pursuing self-appointed goals is an unargued causal claim with obvious competing explanations — and the chapter itself supplies a different one two pages earlier, where modern unhappiness is traced to a want of **emotional security** rather than to permissiveness. ⚠️ The legitimate use is therefore structural: it shows that communitarianism makes a **consequentialist** case against liberal individualism, predicting social costs rather than merely asserting a different ideal, and it identifies what evidence would in principle bear on the dispute. ⚠️ An answer that presents the argument's form while flagging its unsupported status demonstrates both comprehension and source discipline, which is worth more than either alone.

**Misconception to avoid:** Do not attribute the **unencumbered self**, the original position or any critique of Rawls to this page. The chapter gives Sandel one sentence, in which he **criticizes the liberals' conception of disjointed nature of the people**, views human nature as **'embedded' in a particular time, place and culture**, and calls for a **'deeper commonality'**. Rawls is not named anywhere in the chapter; that material comes from the subject owner's extension and must be carried under its own marker.

## Lesson 21 — Miliband: compromise against cooperation

Progress: 21 / 24 | Stage: Advanced | Subtopic: The two quoted passages, the chapter's own verdict on liberalism, and functional against dysfunctional conflict

━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

🖼️ **VISUAL — one distinction, and the verdict the chapter delivers in its own voice**

```text
   THE THREE QUESTIONS that separate liberal from Marxist (p.105)
     (1) "the NATURE and SOURCE of the conflict from which
          politics emanates"
     (2) "the PROSPECTS OF CONFLICT-RESOLUTION"
     (3) "the UTILITY OF POLITICS ITSELF"
                              |
   RALPH MILIBAND (Marxism and Politics; 1977), as quoted:
   "In the LIBERAL view of politics, conflict exists in terms of
    'PROBLEMS' which need to be 'SOLVED'. The HIDDEN ASSUMPTION
    is that conflict DOES NOT, OR NEED NOT, RUN VERY DEEP; that
    it can be 'MANAGED' by the exercise of REASON AND GOOD WILL
    and A READINESS TO COMPROMISE AND AGREE . . . The MARXIST
    approach to conflict is VERY DIFFERENT. It is NOT a matter of
    'problems' to be 'solved' but of A STATE OF DOMINATION AND

    conditions which give rise to it."
                              |
   THE CHAPTER'S OWN VERDICT  -- not Miliband's, the chapter's
   "APPARENTLY, the liberal approach SEEMS to be based on 'REASON'
    and devoted to 'JUSTICE'. BUT THE DIFFICULTY with this
    approach is that it FAILS TO DISTINGUISH BETWEEN 'COMPROMISE'
    AND 'COOPERATION'."
                              |
   THE DISTINCTION, stated precisely                    (p.106)
   "'COMPROMISE' CAN BE EFFECTED WITHOUT CREATING THE CONDITIONS
    OF 'FREEDOM'."
   "Real 'COOPERATION' among members of different groups or
    classes CAN BE GENERATED ONLY AFTER ELIMINATION OF THE
    CONDITIONS OF 'DOMINANCE' AND 'DEPENDENCE'."
   "Liberal writers USE 'COMPROMISE' AND 'COOPERATION'
    SYNONYMOUSLY WHICH OBSCURES THE WHOLE ISSUE."
                              |
   THE SECOND MILIBAND PASSAGE -- the liberal case restated
   conflict "is NOT ONLY CIVILIZED, BUT ALSO CIVILIZING"
   it is "not only a means of RESOLVING PROBLEMS in a peaceful
    way, but also of providing NEW IDEAS, ensuring PROGRESS,
    achieving EVER-GREATER HARMONY"
   => conflict is "'FUNCTIONAL', a STABILIZING rather than a
      'DISRUPTING FORCE'"
                              |
   AGAINST which:
   Marxist theory "regards conflict as 'DYSFUNCTIONAL'. It is
    SYMPTOMATIC OF A RIFT within society which tends to PERPETUATE
    THE PRACTICE OF EXPLOITATION until its character is
    TRANSFORMED BY A 'SOCIALIST' REVOLUTION."
   "The so-called resolution of conflict by liberal methods is in
    reality A POOR CONSOLATION: 'The antagonisms are
    IRRECONCILABLE, and the notion of GENUINE HARMONY IS A
    DECEPTION OR A DELUSION, AT LEAST IN RELATION TO CLASS
    SOCIETIES'."
```

*Caption: this is the one place where the chapter stops reporting and delivers a verdict of its own. The compromise/cooperation distinction is its own contribution, and it is the most useful single tool the topic supplies.*

### 1. The three questions, and Miliband's contrast

✅ **The chief difference between the liberal and the Marxist points of view regarding the nature of politics lies in the questions of the nature and source of the conflict from which politics emanates, the prospects of conflict-resolution, and the utility of politics itself.**

⚠️ **Three questions, and the third is the one that makes this chapter's business distinctive.** ⚠️ **The utility of politics itself** asks whether politics is **worth having** — a question neither Lesson 3's definition nor Lesson 5's Easton raises, and one on which the Marxist answer is that politics is an artefact of class division to be dispensed with.

> In the liberal view of politics, conflict exists in terms of 'problems' which need to be 'solved'. The hidden assumption is that conflict does not, or need not, run very deep; that it can be 'managed' by the exercise of reason and good will and a readiness to compromise and agree . . . The Marxist approach to conflict is very different. It is not a matter of 'problems' to be 'solved' but of a state of domination and subjection to be ended by a total transformation of the conditions which give rise to it.
>
> — Ralph Miliband, *Marxism and Politics* (1977), as quoted by Gauba, Ch.3, PDF p.105 (the ellipsis is the chapter's)

⚠️ ⚠️ **The passage works by contrasting vocabularies, and that is the analytical lesson.** ⚠️ The liberal speaks of **problems** that are **solved** and **managed**; the Marxist of **a state of domination and subjection** to be **ended by a total transformation**. ⚠️ ⚠️ A **problem** is something a situation **has**, which can be removed leaving the situation intact. ⚠️ A **state of domination** is something a situation **is**, which cannot be removed without transforming the situation. ⚠️ So the word choice encodes the disagreement, and Miliband's phrase **the hidden assumption** says the liberal vocabulary smuggles in a conclusion about depth without arguing for it.

⚠️ **Note also the liberal's three named resources: "reason and good will and a readiness to compromise and agree".** ⚠️ Two are dispositions rather than capacities. ⚠️ The Marxist objection is not that these are absent but that they are **insufficient** against a structural condition.

### 2. The chapter's own verdict

⚠️ ⚠️ **This is the chapter speaking for itself, and it should be identified as such.** ⚠️ **Apparently, the liberal approach seems to be based on 'reason' and devoted to 'justice'. But the difficulty with this approach is that it fails to distinguish between 'compromise' and 'cooperation'.** ⚠️ **Apparently** and **seems** are both concessive, and **the difficulty** is the chapter's own assessment rather than a reported one.

✅ **The distinction, in two sentences:** **'Compromise' can be effected without creating the conditions of 'freedom'.** **Real 'cooperation' among members of different groups or classes can be generated only after elimination of the conditions of 'dominance' and 'dependence'.** ✅ And the charge: **liberal writers use 'compromise' and 'cooperation' synonymously which obscures the whole issue.**

⚠️ ⚠️ **This is the most useful analytical tool in the topic, and it should be set out as a test.**

| | **Compromise** | **Cooperation** |
|---|---|---|
| **Precondition** | ⚠️ None — it **can be effected without creating the conditions of 'freedom'** | **Only after elimination of the conditions of 'dominance' and 'dependence'** |
| **What it shows** | That the parties settled | That the parties were **free** to settle |
| **Observable difference?** | ⚠️ ⚠️ No — both look like agreement from outside | |

⚠️ ⚠️ **The test's power is that it redirects attention from the settlement to its conditions**, and this is where it cashes in Lesson 4's **pressure**. ⚠️ Lesson 4 listed **pressure** among the methods qualifying as political solutions **short of war**. ⚠️ Lesson 14 required both parties to have **their own organizations**. ⚠️ Miliband's distinction says that a settlement reached by pressure between unequally placed parties is a **compromise** and not **cooperation**, however amicable it appears — and that calling it cooperation **obscures the whole issue**.

⚠️ 🔶 **The owner makes this the formal distinction for exam use**, listing **compromise / cooperation** among the advanced distinctions and noting that Gauba, via Miliband, holds that liberal compromise **can occur without removing domination**.

### 3. Functional against dysfunctional

✅ Per the liberal view as Miliband elaborates it, conflict **is not only civilized, but also civilizing**: it is **not only a means of resolving problems in a peaceful way, but also of providing new ideas, ensuring progress, achieving ever-greater harmony, and so on**, so that conflict is **'functional', a stabilizing rather than a 'disrupting force'**.

✅ Against this, **Marxist theory regards conflict as 'dysfunctional'. It is symptomatic of a rift within society which tends to perpetuate the practice of exploitation until its character is transformed by a 'socialist' revolution.** ✅ And **the so-called resolution of conflict by liberal methods is in reality a poor consolation: 'The antagonisms are irreconcilable, and the notion of genuine harmony is a deception or a delusion, at least in relation to class societies'.**

⚠️ ⚠️ **"Civilized, but also civilizing" is the single best summary of the liberal position on conflict and is worth memorising.** ⚠️ **Civilized** says the conflict is conducted within rules; **civilizing** says the conflict **improves** the parties. ⚠️ The second is the stronger and more surprising claim — conflict is not merely tolerable but **productive**, which is exactly Lesson 15's mechanism in which awareness of conflict and **their interaction** generates awareness of the common interest.

⚠️ **Functional against dysfunctional is a borrowed sociological pair** and the chapter uses it without explanation. ⚠️ **Functional** means contributing to the maintenance of the system; **dysfunctional**, tending to its breakdown. ⚠️ ⚠️ Note the paradox the liberal position embraces: conflict is a **stabilizing rather than a 'disrupting force'**, so the very thing that looks like disorder is what keeps the order going.

⚠️ ⚠️ **And note the hedge in the quoted Marxist conclusion: "at least in relation to class societies".** ⚠️ The claim that **genuine harmony is a deception or a delusion** is explicitly **restricted** to class societies. ⚠️ That restriction is what keeps the Marxist position coherent with its own prediction that a classless society would achieve genuine harmony. ❌ Dropping it converts a conditional claim into a universal denial of harmony, which the text does not support.

⚠️ ❌ **Scan-artefact disclosure.** The extraction prints **is*'functional'** with a stray asterisk, normalised and catalogued.

Revision notes:
- ✅ The liberal-Marxist difference lies in **the nature and source of the conflict**, **the prospects of conflict-resolution**, and **the utility of politics itself**.
- ✅ **Ralph Miliband** (*Marxism and Politics*; **1977**): liberals treat conflict as **'problems' which need to be 'solved'**, on **the hidden assumption** that it **does not, or need not, run very deep** and can be **'managed' by the exercise of reason and good will and a readiness to compromise and agree**; the Marxist approach treats it as **a state of domination and subjection to be ended by a total transformation of the conditions which give rise to it**.
- ⚠️ A **problem** is what a situation **has**; **domination** is what a situation **is**. The vocabulary encodes the disagreement.
- ✅ ⚠️ The chapter's **own** verdict: **apparently** the liberal approach **seems** based on **reason** and devoted to **justice**, **but the difficulty with this approach is that it fails to distinguish between 'compromise' and 'cooperation'**.
- ✅ **'Compromise' can be effected without creating the conditions of 'freedom'.** ✅ **Real 'cooperation' … can be generated only after elimination of the conditions of 'dominance' and 'dependence'.** ✅ Liberal writers **use 'compromise' and 'cooperation' synonymously which obscures the whole issue**.
- ⚠️ ⚠️ The test redirects attention from the settlement to its **conditions** — and cashes in Lesson 4's **pressure** and Lesson 14's organisational symmetry.
- ✅ Liberal view: conflict **is not only civilized, but also civilizing**, providing **new ideas**, **ensuring progress**, **achieving ever-greater harmony**; it is **'functional', a stabilizing rather than a 'disrupting force'**.
- ✅ Marxist view: conflict is **'dysfunctional'**, **symptomatic of a rift** that **tends to perpetuate the practice of exploitation** until transformed by a **'socialist' revolution**; liberal resolution is **a poor consolation**, since **the antagonisms are irreconcilable, and the notion of genuine harmony is a deception or a delusion, at least in relation to class societies**.
- ⚠️ Keep **at least in relation to class societies** — the denial of harmony is **conditional**.

### Concept check

**Question:** Why is the compromise/cooperation distinction the most useful analytical tool this chapter supplies?

**Model answer:** Because it converts an unresolvable dispute about what a settlement **is** into a checkable question about the **conditions** under which it was reached. ⚠️ Lesson 17 left liberal and Marxist accounts describing the same events — settlements, agreements, policies — and disagreeing about whether they were genuine reconciliations or **a compromise imposed by the dominant class**, with the **illusion of consent** blocking any observational test. ⚠️ The chapter's own distinction breaks that deadlock by relocating the question: **'compromise' can be effected without creating the conditions of 'freedom'**, whereas real **'cooperation' … can be generated only after elimination of the conditions of 'dominance' and 'dependence'**. ⚠️ So instead of asking whether the parties agreed — which both sides concede — one asks whether the parties were **free and non-dependent** when they agreed, and that is a question about standing social conditions rather than about hidden mental states. ⚠️ The tool also has reach backwards into the chapter: Lesson 4 admitted **pressure** among the methods short of war, and Lesson 14's liberal picture assumed that both sides possess **their own organizations**, so the distinction tests exactly the premises the liberal account needs. ⚠️ And it is the chapter's own verdict rather than a reported one, introduced by **the difficulty with this approach is**, with the charge that liberal writers **use 'compromise' and 'cooperation' synonymously which obscures the whole issue**. ⚠️ The residual to concede is that the chapter supplies no threshold — how much inequality constitutes **dominance** is not specified — so the test sharpens the question without finally settling it, which is why the chapter ends at Lesson 22 with scope conditions rather than a verdict.

**Misconception to avoid:** Do not quote **the notion of genuine harmony is a deception or a delusion** without its qualifier **at least in relation to class societies**. The restriction is essential to the position's coherence, since the Marxist prediction is precisely that a **classless** society achieves what class society cannot; dropping it turns a conditional claim about one kind of society into a universal denial that harmony is ever possible, which the text does not say.
