Three contemporary critics are named — MacIntyre on virtue **through cooperative human activity** aimed at **standards of excellence**, Taylor against the **'atomistic' conception**, Sandel for a **'deeper commonality' informed by 'shared self-understanding' as well as affection** (Lesson 20). ⚠️ The chapter's verdict is conditional and includes one severe judgment: **none of these views of politics may be treated as universally acceptable**, the communitarian view suiting a society whose members are **largely satisfied with their status** and **prepared to cooperate**, and being **futile** where there are **wide disparities of power and differences of opinion** (Lesson 22). ⚠️ The caricature is avoided by three textual facts: communitarianism **focuses on** duties rather than denying rights; the state is an **instrument** whose end is **good life for everyone**; and the chapter supplies the view's failure condition itself.

**Misconception to avoid:** Do not read **does not recognize even their right to pursue self-interest apart from the interest of the community itself** as abolishing self-interest or rights. **Apart from** is load-bearing, and the following page confirms it by objecting to applying one's potentialities **disregarding the interest of society**. The position conditions the pursuit of self-interest; it does not prohibit it.

### Cumulative check E — 🔶 Lesson 23 (owner extensions)

**Question:** What do the owner's extensions supply that the chapter cannot, and what discipline governs their use?

**Model answer:** They supply four things, each with a stated limit. 🔶 **Differentiated mechanisms**: the chapter gives MacIntyre, Taylor and Sandel one sentence each, while the owner insists they must not be **one undifferentiated voice** and supplies **practices** with **internal goods** judgeable **only from within** a tradition; the **dialogical** self and **recognition**, whose withholding **inflicts real harm**; and the **unencumbered self** behind Rawls's **veil of ignorance**, against which **real persons are partly constituted by their ends and attachments**. 🔶 **Precursor discipline**: Rousseau and Green are **precursors/anticipations, not card-carrying communitarians**, correcting a possible over-reading of the chapter's own Exponents row. 🔶 **Two objection-reply chains**: the neutrality objection with the Rawlsian reply that neutrality is **a political device for fair terms of cooperation**, and the unencumbered-self objection with the reply that the veil is **a heuristic device** and the counter-reply that it **smuggles in an individualist picture of practical reasoning**. 🔶 **Hedged Indian anchors**: Gandhi as **analogy** only, the Fundamental Rights / Directive Principles / Fundamental Duties balance as **structure** without article numbers, and reservation debates as a **communitarian-adjacent** type of argument without statistics. ⚠️ The discipline is a three-tier provenance rule: Tier 1 is PDF pp.94-107, cited by page; Tier 2 is this extension material, marked 🔶 and never cited to Gauba — note especially that **Rawls is not named in the chapter at all**; Tier 3 is outside both and is not used. ⚠️ The owner's quotation method applies throughout: **named propositions rather than unverified direct quotations**.

**Misconception to avoid:** Do not attribute the unencumbered self, the original position or any Rawls critique to Gauba's chapter. The chapter's Sandel sentence says only that he **criticizes the liberals' conception of disjointed nature of the people**, views human nature as **'embedded' in a particular time, place and culture**, and calls for a **'deeper commonality'**. Rawls appears nowhere in the chapter.

### Cumulative check F — the whole topic

**Question:** Two minutes before a 20-mark answer comparing the three views. List the checks you run and what each prevents.

**Model answer:** First, **decode the directive** and fix the structure — a comparison needs the ontology, the three answers, an objection-reply chain and a graded verdict; this prevents a list. Second, run the **ontology check**: name the image of society first, the **Set of Independent Individuals**, the **Set of Antagonistic Classes** and the **Set of Intellectually and Emotionally United Human Beings**, because each account of politics is derived from its account of society; this prevents an inverted answer that begins with institutions. Third, run the **provenance check** across the three tiers — chapter, 🔶 owner extension, outside both — remembering that Rawls, practices, recognition, the precursor discipline and every Indian anchor are extension; this prevents attributing to a 2009 textbook material it lacks. Fourth, run the **boundary check**: no Topic 04 Marxist apparatus, no Aristotelian theory beyond the organ-organism analogy, no Rawls reconstruction, no Weberian or systems machinery, no constitutional articles, no post-2009 fact, and the **USSR (1917)** and **China (1949)** references carried as **book-period illustrations**; this prevents unverifiable assertion. Fifth, run the **caricature check** on communitarianism — **focuses on** duties, the state as **instrument**, the **futility** clause — since the source itself forbids the collectivist reading. Sixth, select **evidence units with their limits attached**: the hinge sentence on social process, the public/universal filter, **war is a symptom of the failure**, Easton's three terms, the two tax directions, the always-claim, the two-way conditional, Engels's medium-of-the-state mechanism, the define/discover pair, the compromise/cooperation test, the futility clause. Seventh, choose an **objection-reply chain** and concede its residual. Then write, closing on the chapter's own conditional verdict rather than crowning a winner.

**Misconception to avoid:** Do not end a comparative answer by declaring one view correct. The chapter's own conclusion is that **none of these views of politics may be treated as universally acceptable**, and it matches each to a type of society — low disparity, sharp division with no peaceful alternative, and broad satisfaction with willingness to cooperate. A graded conditional verdict is the chapter's position, and reproducing it is more accurate than improving on it.

# ORIGINAL 10-, 15- AND 20-MARK MAINS MODEL PRACTICE

⚠️ The questions below are original practice prompts written for this session. They are not previous-year questions and must not be cited as such.

### 10-mark practice (~150 words)

**Practice question 1 [10]:** What makes a conflict political rather than merely private? *(What — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Model answer:** Two filters, applied to a conflict that already involves disagreement and efforts at resolution through the authority of government. The first is publicness: only a conflict arising at a public level counts, meaning one in which two or more major groups are involved, and it must demand a universal solution, not a private one. ⚠️ Scale and subject matter are explicitly not criteria, since a qualifying conflict may arise in any social setting, local to international, and on the economic, cultural, linguistic, religious or ethnic plane. The second filter excludes war, because a political solution emanates from negotiation, persuasion, arbitration, compromise, pressure or counting of votes, and war is a symptom of the failure of a political solution. ⚠️ The decisive test is the remedy: a husband-and-wife quarrel becomes political once posed as a question of respective claims of men and women demanding solution at the public level.

**Scoring note for this answer:** the marks sit in the two filters stated as filters rather than as a definition, and in the closing conversion case, which shows the test being applied rather than recited. Noting that scale and subject matter are **not** criteria pre-empts the commonest error.

**Practice question 2 [10]:** Why does the chapter call war "a symptom of the failure of a political solution" rather than simply non-political? *(Why — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Model answer:** Because the two claims differ in what they concede. Calling war non-political would sever it from the subject entirely, whereas the chapter concedes that some political disputes do culminate in war — so war arises out of political disputes and is intelligible only by reference to them. ⚠️ What is denied is narrower: that resort to violence or war qualifies as a political solution, since the qualifying methods are negotiation, persuasion, arbitration, compromise, pressure and counting of votes, all short of war. ⚠️ The formulation does three things. It keeps war inside the explanatory scope of political science as an outcome to be explained. It supplies a criterion of failure, so any settlement can be assessed on politics' own terms. And it coheres with the acceptability requirement, since a settlement is supposed to be acceptable to the parties, whereas force secures compliance without acceptability.

**Scoring note for this answer:** a Why question fails on description, so the answer contrasts the chapter's formulation with the stronger alternative it declined, then names the three jobs it does. Connecting the exclusion to the acceptability clause shows the chapter read as a connected argument.

### 15-mark practice (~200-250 words)

**Practice question 3 [15]:** Examine the claim that communitarianism is a form of collectivism hostile to individual rights. *(Examine — answer in 200 to 250 words.)*

**Model answer:** Stated at full strength, the claim is that a doctrine deriving the person from the community must subordinate that person to it. There is surface warrant: communitarianism holds that the individual owes his existence and his potentialities to society, that his commitment to society is an essential feature of his personality, and that it does not recognise even a right to pursue self-interest apart from the interest of the community.

The claim nonetheless fails on three grounds. First, textual: the chapter says communitarianism focuses on duties and obligations where liberalism insists on rights — a difference of emphasis, and nowhere a denial of rights. Second, structural: the state is described as an indispensable instrument of good life for all human beings, so it is a means, and the end is distributed across everyone — the reverse of the fascist relation in which the state is an end and the individual a means. Third, the operative qualifier: what is denied is a right to pursue self-interest **apart from** the community's interest, the following page glossing this as disregarding the interest of society.

Attacking the logic: the inference runs from a claim about the **origin** of the self to a claim about **authority** over it, which does not follow.

⚠️ Finding: the claim fails, but a residual stands — the chapter supplies its safeguards by formulation rather than argument, and the advanced owner warns that fraternity detached from liberty and equality can slide into conformity.

**Scoring note for this answer:** "Examine" needs the claim at full strength, evidence both ways and a finding; the distinguishing moves are the logical attack on the origin-to-authority inference and the conceded residual, which converts a defence into an assessment.

**Practice question 4 [15]:** "Liberal politics reconciles interests; Marxist politics suppresses them." Critically examine. *(Critically examine — answer in 200 to 250 words.)*

**Model answer:** The proposition accurately reports both positions and conceals what is at stake between them.

It is accurate. The liberal view holds that politics is an instrument of reconciliation of the conflicting interests in society, that conflict is not very deep, and that some way can always be found to reconcile it. The Marxist view holds that class interests are irreconcilable, that politics cannot resolve the conflict, and that politics is used by the dominant class to suppress it.

What it conceals is that both describe the **same events**. Settlements occur on either account; the dispute is over what they are — genuine reconciliation, or a compromise imposed by the dominant class which merely gives the impression of voluntary compromise. ⚠️ And the Marxist adds a mechanism that blocks observational testing: politics creates an illusion of consent by administering strong ideological doses, so observed acceptance cannot settle the question.

Attacking the logic: a contrast stated as two descriptions suggests an empirical disagreement, where the disagreement is interpretive.

⚠️ The chapter's own resolution is a third term. Liberalism fails to distinguish between compromise and cooperation: compromise can be effected without creating the conditions of freedom, while real cooperation requires elimination of the conditions of dominance and dependence.

⚠️ Verdict: the proposition holds as reportage and fails as analysis; the real question is about conditions, not outcomes.

**Scoring note for this answer:** the critical element comes from showing that a true statement can still be analytically inadequate, and the lift is in deploying the chapter's own compromise/cooperation distinction as the resolution rather than importing an external criterion.

### 20-mark practice (~250-300 words)

**Practice question 5 [20]:** Critically evaluate the claim that politics is essentially an instrument of conflict-resolution. *(Critically evaluate — answer in 250 to 300 words.)*

**Model answer:** Evaluation needs a criterion. The one adopted is whether the claim can accommodate all three accounts of politics the chapter recognises.

The claim has strong support in the chapter's own apparatus. A political situation presupposes a disagreement or conflict and efforts for its resolution through the authority of government, and the end-product is the resolution of the conflict from which the process had started. On the liberal account the claim is simply true: politics is an instrument of reconciliation, and any conflict is capable of resolution by conciliation and legitimate coercion.

Against it, two accounts resist. On the Marxist view politics cannot resolve the conflict; it is used by the dominant class to suppress it, so politics is a symptom of class division rather than its remedy, and the end of class-conflict marks an end of politics itself. On the communitarian view there is no real conflict in the society, the impression of it arising from misunderstanding of our real interest, so politics is redefined as the pursuit of the common good rather than resolution of some pre-existing conflict.

⚠️ Decisively, the chapter concedes the point in its own hedge: resolution is only what the end-product is **supposed to be**, and on this point there is a divergence between liberal and Marxist viewpoints.

Attacking the logic: the word **essentially** claims a defining property, which a single coherent rival account defeats — and there are two.

⚠️ Graded verdict: conflict-resolution is the liberal **conception** of politics, not its essence. The chapter's own position is conditional — none of these views may be treated as universally acceptable — and each is matched to a type of society.

**Scoring note for this answer:** "Critically evaluate" fails on an unnamed criterion, so it is declared first and every judgment refers back. The decisive move is using the chapter's own hedge, **supposed to be**, as the evidence against the claim — an internal refutation is worth more than an imported one.

**Practice question 6 [20]:** "The three views of politics differ about institutions." Discuss. *(Discuss — answer in 250 to 300 words.)*

**Model answer:** The proposition is plausible because the three views do recommend different institutional arrangements, and it must be stated before it is tested.

There is evidence for it. The liberal view looks to rules and policies — a reasonable price, a queue system, a red and green signal system — and terminates in the modern welfare state. The Marxist view looks to social ownership of the means of production and, after revolution, to a state that will wither away. The communitarian view looks to arrangements sustaining shared understanding and belonging.

The proposition nonetheless fails, because these recommendations are **consequences** of a prior disagreement about what society is. The chapter's summary table states it directly: society is a Set of Independent Individuals, a Set of Antagonistic Classes, or a Set of Intellectually and Emotionally United Human Beings. From each ontology the account of politics follows — reconciliation of conflicting interests, suppression of class conflict, pursuit of the common good — and only then the institutions.

⚠️ The depth of the disagreement shows in the common good itself: a Stage of Equilibrium between Conflicting Interests for the liberal, a Uniform Good that Comprehends the Interest of Each Member for the communitarian. Both use the same words for incompatible things.

Attacking the logic: inferring a disagreement about institutions from differing institutional recommendations mistakes the output for the dispute.

⚠️ Verdict: the proposition is true at the surface and false as an account of what divides the three. The owner's instruction captures it — identify the implicit social ontology before discussing institutions or policy — and the chapter's conditional verdict confirms it, since each view is matched to a type of **society**, not to a type of institution.

**Scoring note for this answer:** a Discuss stem requires both sides and a verdict, and the distinguishing move is the logical attack — naming the inference from differing outputs to a disagreement about outputs as a mistake — then demonstrating the real disagreement with the common-good row, where identical vocabulary conceals incompatible content.

# REMEDIATION

| # | Error | Correction | Taught in |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Treating "politics" as having one sense | It **is applied to a particular social phenomenon as well as to a systematic study of that phenomenon**; this topic is about the **activity** | L1 |
| 2 | Reporting Ernest Benn's definition as the chapter's view | ❌ It is introduced as **sarcastic** and sits inside the catalogue of lay impressions the chapter sets out to **dispel** | L1 |
| 3 | Conflating Ball's "public sector" with the chapter's "public issues" | Ball objects to confining politics to **parliaments, elections, cabinets**; the chapter's filter is about **two or more major groups** and a **universal solution** — a test of who is affected, not where | L1, L4 |
| 4 | Presenting the traditional "science of the state" as merely descriptive | It had **two** tasks — **a study of the institutions of the state** and **developing ideas concerning the nature of a perfect state** | L2 |
| 5 | Missing the hinge of the whole chapter | Politics **does not operate strictly within the framework of an institutional set-up but permeates the entire social fabric**, and so is **a social process rather than an aggregate of the formal institutions of the state** | L2 |
| 6 | Treating Ball, Wasby and Miller as rival definitions | They stack: conflict-and-resolution, the absence clause, and **the presence of government as a means** | L3 |
| 7 | Reading politics as inherently about change | Miller's formula is **in the direction of change or in the prevention of change** — both are political, which is what lets the Marxist suppression account count | L3 |
| 8 | Accepting Ball's nursery example | ❌ The chapter rejects it on **two defects** — it is **private**, and it contemplates **resort to violence** | L4 |
| 9 | Treating scale or subject matter as a test of publicness | Both lists **widen**: **local, regional, national or international**, and **economic, cultural, linguistic, religious or ethnic** | L4 |
| 10 | Saying war is non-political | **Some political disputes do culminate in war, but war is a symptom of the failure of a political solution** — a failure mode, not an exclusion from the subject | L4 |
| 11 | Forgetting **pressure** among the qualifying methods | It is listed **short of war**, which is the opening Lesson 21's compromise/cooperation test exploits | L4, L21 |
| 12 | Glossing Easton's "values" as moral values or material goods | **things considered valuable, whether they be spiritual or material** | L5 |
| 13 | Treating policy as a single decision | **Policy … consists of a 'web of decisions'**, and **policy implies arriving at a decision as well as its implementation** | L5 |
| 14 | Defining authority by the ruler's capacity | It is defined by the subjects' attitude — **they must or ought to obey it** — and negatively as **not … the use of brute force** but **the capacity to secure more-or-less willing compliance** | L5 |
| 15 | Smoothing over the authority/force tension | ⚠️ A policy can be authoritative **purely from fear of consequences** while authority **does not signify the use of brute force**; the chapter never draws the line. Any reconstruction must be labelled | L5, L10 |
| 16 | Quoting Roberts as a neutral definition | ⚠️ The chapter labels the cooperation clause **taking a liberal view of politics** | L6 |
| 17 | Stating the politics/economics discriminator symmetrically | It is asymmetric: economics covers **public or private** allocation; politics is **exclusively** concerned with the **public**, and **in pursuance of 'public goals'** | L6 |
| 18 | Treating politicization as a yes/no property | It is a **ratio** against a stated population — Greek city-states were high among **freemen** while excluding **the very large majority of slaves and aliens** | L7 |
| 19 | Dropping "intended" from the democracy claim | **It is only under a democracy that politicization is intended to be universal** | L7 |
| 20 | Building an account of caste politics on this chapter | ❌ The entire treatment is the parenthesis **(e.g. politicization of caste)**. ➡️ Caste owners | L7 |
| 21 | Treating scarcity as sufficient for politics | ⚠️ Where people are **complacent or ascetic** there is **hardly any possibility of conflict**; the third condition is **desired by many** | L8 |
| 22 | Presenting the nine values as a closed taxonomy | **Some of the examples**; and **deference** is positional, so some conflicts cannot be dissolved by producing more | L8 |
| 23 | Missing how a private quarrel converts | ⚠️ The **facts** do not change; the **description** does — it **takes the form of a question of respective claims of men and women** | L9 |
| 24 | Drawing a substantive gender claim from the conversion case | ❌ It illustrates a **logical** conversion only. ➡️ Topic 06 and the Gender Discrimination owner | L9 |
| 25 | Reading Curtis's two markers as one test | They are joined by **or**: **concern a society as a totality** **or** **may ultimately be resolved by the exercise of legitimate coercion** | L9 |
| 26 | Citing only one tax example | ⚠️ The chapter gives **two**, running in opposite directions, and binds both by the same reason — **the poor, too, cannot refuse** | L10 |
| 27 | Missing the asymmetry between the two | ⚠️ Only Direction A is given a legitimating belief; Direction B notes that police and defence are **chiefly meant for the protection of the property of the rich** with no belief supplied | L10 |
| 28 | Quoting the reservation and tax examples as Indian law or policy | ❌ **2009** illustrations; no article, statute, rate or date is given. ➡️ Polity | L10 |
| 29 | Omitting the revisability guard | **No authoritative allocation can be treated as eternal or unalterable. It holds the field until a new arrangement is established as authoritative** | L10 |
| 30 | Treating parties as the primary political actors | Demands **emanate from** interest groups; parties **appear on the scene** afterwards to formulate **large policy proposals** | L11 |
| 31 | Dropping "supposed to be" from the fifth characteristic | ⚠️ The hedge is what preserves the Marxist view as a rival rather than defining it out of existence | L11 |
| 32 | Building an account of political culture or political economy here | ❌ Both are derived in one sentence and dropped — no definition, components or typology | L12 |
| 33 | Importing a full systems model | ❌ The chapter uses only the nesting relation. ➡️ The approaches owner | L12, L14 |
| 34 | Presenting communitarianism as a third answer to the same question | ⚠️ The chapter's word is **redefines** — it denies the shared premise that a pre-existing conflict is the datum | L13 |
| 35 | Converting "focuses on duties" into "rejects rights" | ❌ **While liberalism insists on the rights of the individual, communitarianism focuses on his duties and obligations** is a difference of **emphasis**; the advanced owner forbids the stronger reading | L13, L19 |
| 36 | Citing the loneliness diagnosis as a finding | ❌ No evidence, study or source is offered — it is the doctrine's **motivating claim** | L13 |
| 37 | Treating Rousseau and Green as communitarians | They appear under **early indications**; 🔶 the owner requires **precursors/anticipations, not card-carrying communitarians** | L13, 🔶 L23 |
| 38 | Missing the cross-cutting ground of liberal pluralism | ⚠️ Individuals act **as members of different groups** — plural, hence cross-cutting. ⚠️ A reconstruction, not the chapter's statement | L14 |
| 39 | Treating "demands and supports" as one-way | **Supports** are glossed as **obedience to laws and regulations, payment of taxes** — the circuit runs both ways | L14 |
| 40 | Dropping the liberal always-claim's qualifiers | **Apparent clash**; **some way can always be found**; **you can always form a rule** — a universal claim, refutable by one counter-example | L15 |
| 41 | Presenting the liberal view as purely consensual | It retains coercion: **capable of resolution by conciliation and legitimate coercion** | L15 |
| 42 | Confusing the two common goods | Liberal: **Stage of Equilibrium between Conflicting Interests**, reached **through** conflict. Communitarian: **Uniform Good that Comprehends the Interest of Each Member**, **only to be discovered and identified** | L15, L19, L22 |
| 43 | Citing the queue and traffic-signal cases as proof of the always-claim | ⚠️ Both are **coordination** problems with no opposed interest; they do not test the strong thesis | L15 |
| 44 | Importing Topic 04's Marxist apparatus | ❌ The chapter names the **mode of production** once and develops no historical materialism or base-superstructure vocabulary. ➡️ Topic 04 | L16 |
| 45 | Stating only one half of the two-way conditional | Both: **if the economic issue is solved, all other issues will automatically disappear**; and if evaded, settlement is **a futile exercise** | L16 |
| 46 | Missing that technology attacks scarcity | ⚠️ **Highest advancement of technology so as to get maximum production** attacks Lesson 8's **rarity** condition — the Marxist route to the end of politics runs through the chapter's own definition | L8, L16 |
| 47 | Reading Engels's state as a passive reflection | It **through the medium of the state, becomes also the politically dominant class, and thus acquires new means of holding down and exploiting** — a converter and amplifier. ⚠️ Keep **as a rule** | L17 |
| 48 | Quoting the Manifesto without its second disjunct | **Either in a revolutionary reconstitution of society at large, or in the common ruin of the contending classes** | L17 |
| 49 | Treating the illusion of consent as settled | ⚠️ It blocks observational testing and is **unfalsifiable as stated**; the chapter supplies no criterion for distinguishing genuine from illusory consent | L17 |
| 50 | Missing that the withering-away thesis covers politics too | **State and politics would no longer be required**; **the end of class-conflict marks an end of politics itself** — which collides with Easton's **unavoidable** allocation | L5, L17 |
| 51 | Reading the communitarian claim as optimism | It is a claim about **nature**: **the essence of human nature lies in the spirit of cooperation, not of conflict** | L18 |
| 52 | Pressing the organ-organism analogy to collectivism | ❌ The chapter makes the state **an indispensable instrument of good life for all human beings** and distributes the end across **everyone** | L18 |
| 53 | Reconstructing Aristotle from this chapter | ❌ One analogy and one conclusion; no *polis*, *telos*, virtue theory, work or dates. ➡️ Aristotle's owners | L18 |
| 54 | Missing the define/discover pair | Liberals **define and seek** their own good; communitarians **discover and pursue** it — hence one can be **mistaken** about one's own good | L19 |
| 55 | Dropping "apart from" | ⚠️ What is denied is a right to pursue self-interest **apart from** the community's interest, glossed as **disregarding the interest of society** | L19 |
| 56 | Ignoring "well-functioning" | ⚠️ Flourishing requires **a place in a well-functioning community** — the chapter's only internal concession that communities can function badly | L19 |
| 57 | Citing MacIntyre's disintegration claim as evidence | ❌ **Crime and violence, the breakdown of the family, and the widespread drug abuse** in **some modern liberal states** — no study, country, period or data; a **2009** assertion, and the causal attribution is unargued | L20 |
| 58 | Attributing Rawls or the unencumbered self to the chapter | ❌ Rawls is **not named anywhere** in the chapter. 🔶 Owner extension | L20, 🔶 L23 |
| 59 | Treating the three communitarians as interchangeable | 🔶 **Practices/tradition** (MacIntyre) · **recognition/dialogical self** (Taylor) · **unencumbered self/original position** (Sandel); a generic answer **loses specific marks** | L20, 🔶 L23 |
| 60 | Missing "as well as affection" in Sandel | It matches the table's **Intellectually and Emotionally United Human Beings** | L20, L22 |
| 61 | Quoting only "pursuit" from the communitarian summary | Both verbs: **the identification and pursuit of their common interest** — **identification** is what the define/discover pair predicts | L19, L20 |
| 62 | Treating the compromise/cooperation distinction as Miliband's | ⚠️ It is the **chapter's own** verdict, introduced by **the difficulty with this approach is** | L21 |
| 63 | Omitting the conditional on the Marxist harmony claim | **The notion of genuine harmony is a deception or a delusion, at least in relation to class societies** | L21 |
| 64 | Missing the functional/dysfunctional pair | Liberal conflict is **not only civilized, but also civilizing** and **'functional', a stabilizing rather than a 'disrupting force'**; Marxist conflict is **'dysfunctional'** | L21 |
| 65 | Citing the USSR and China references as current evidence | 📰 **The former USSR (1917)** and **the People's Republic of China (1949)** are **book-period illustrations**; the finding is only that a classless society is **extremely difficult to realize** | L22 |
| 66 | Dropping the second condition from the Marxist scope clause | A society **sharply divided into dominant and dependent classes, where dependent class is not left with any alternative for peaceful solution** | L22 |
| 67 | Omitting the communitarian futility clause | ❌ **If a society is characterized by wide disparities of power and differences of opinion, it would be futile to look into its problems from the communitarian point of view** — the only explicit futility condition in the chapter | L22 |

Mill**, **Laski**, **Robert Dahl**, **Lenin**, **Gramsci** and **Mao** appear there only as labels; the chapter supplies no date, argument or discussion for any of them. ⚠️ **Laski** alone is named elsewhere — at p.94, only as the author of **A Grammar of Politics and An Introduction to Politics** | L1, L22 |
| 69 | Treating the three definition boxes as parallel | ⚠️ Only **Equality** is flagged **in the Marxist context**; **Fraternity** is defined as **a sentiment** | L22 |
| 70 | Missing that the Liberty box contains MacIntyre's target | **Fulfilling his self-appointed goals** is the exact phrase the disintegration warning attacks | L20, L22 |
| 71 | 🔶 Presenting MacIntyre's position without its limit | 🔶 It **risks relativism**, and his answer — traditions compared by their **capacity to resolve their own internal crises** — **remains contested** | 🔶 L23 |

## Map 1 — The chapter's own summary table, with its derivation made explicit

| The Issue | **Liberal View** | **Marxist View** | **Communitarian View** |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Structure of Society** ⚠️ *(ask this first)* | **Set of Independent Individuals** | **Set of Antagonistic Classes** | **Set of Intellectually and Emotionally United Human Beings** |
| **Status of the Individuals** | **Disconnected, Independent Units** | **Members of Conflicting Classes** | **Mutually connected Units** |
| **Nature of Conflict** | **Not Very Deep which can be Resolved Easily** | **Very Deep Conflict between Classes which cannot be Resolved** | **No Real Conflict within a Community** |
| **Nature of Politics** | **Means of Reconciliation of Conflicting Interests** | **Means of Suppression of class Conflict** | **Means of Pursuit of the Common Good** |
| **Notion of the Common Good** | **Stage of Equilibrium between Conflicting Interests** | **Not Discernible in a Class-Divided Society where Class Interests Dominate** | **Uniform Good that Comprehends the Interest of Each Member of Society** |
| **Way to Progress** | **Conflict-Resolution** | **Revolution** | **Social Solidarity** |
| **Basic Principle** | **Liberty** | **Equality** | **Fraternity** |
| **Exponents** | **Locke, Bentham, J.S. Mill, Laski, Robert Dahl** | **Marx, Engels, Lenin, Gramsci, Mao** | **Rousseau, Green, MacIntyre, Sandel, Charles Taylor** |

# COMPLETE CONSOLIDATED REGISTER NOTES

> Dynamically sized to the material actually taught. Sections follow the order of the chapter; the lesson numbers in each heading let you jump back. Evidence typing follows the convention set out at the head of this file and in the Source Ledger: **bold is ordinary emphasis**, and spans introduced as the chapter's wording are reproduced from the extraction; 🔶 marks owner extensions, which are never cited to the chapter; ❌ marks a boundary.

## §1 — The two senses, and the lay image *(L1; pp.94-95)*

- The chapter's opening sentence: the term **'politics' is applied to a particular social phenomenon as well as to a systematic study of that phenomenon**. This topic is the **phenomenon**; "political science" is the study.
- Ball's **two problems** with general impressions: first, **it is often assumed that politics is only concerned with the public sector, with parliaments, elections, cabinets, and has little relevance to other spheres of human activity**; secondly, **there is the danger of confusing politics solely with party politics**. ⚠️ The extraction prints **contusing**, catalogued.
- The layman's catalogue, as the chapter lists it: **public meetings, processions, slogans, demonstrations, demands, strikes, tear-gas, lathi-charge as well as elections accompanied by false promises and false reports on their fulfilment** — which is why politics gets dubbed the **last resort of scoundrels** and is condemned as a **dirty game**.
- **Ernest Benn** has **sarcastically** described politics as **the art of looking for trouble, finding it where it exists or not, diagnosing it wrongly, and applying the wrong remedy**. ❌ Reproduced as a **sarcastic** description, not endorsed. ⚠️ Note the chapter's version differs from the form usually quoted elsewhere; reproduce **this** one when citing Gauba.
- The chapter's own programme: **for a systematic study of politics it is essential to dispel such confusion and to lift politics from the arena of vague impressions to the level of scientific precision.**
- ⚠️ Why it matters for the exam: a definition question that opens with the popular image and then shows what systematic analysis adds is already half-structured.

## §2 — From the *polis* to the social process *(L2; p.95)*

- Etymology: the word **'politics' itself is derived from the Greek word 'polis' which denoted ancient Greek city-state**; the activities of citizens as **members and operators of the state** were termed **politics**. ⚠️ The extraction prints **polities**, catalogued.
- The traditional discipline: **political science** as the **science of the state**, with **two** devoted tasks — **(a) to a study of the institutions of the state; and (b) to developing ideas concerning the nature of a perfect state**.
- The hinge sentence: **modern writers have increasingly recognized that 'politics' does not operate strictly within the framework of an institutional set-up but permeates the entire social fabric.**
- The relocation: **'politics' is now treated as a social process rather than an aggregate of the formal institutions of the state.**
- ⚠️ The relocation buys reach and creates the chapter's central problem: if politics is everywhere in society, what is **not** politics? Lessons 3-5 answer it.

## §3 — The political situation: three stacked tests *(L3; pp.95-96)*

| Authority | Contribution | The chapter's wording |
|---|---|---|
| **Alan Ball** (*Modern Politics and Government*; 1988) | Conflict **plus** its reconciliation | **political activity … involves disagreements and the reconciliation of those disagreements**; **the essence of the political situation** is **conflict and resolution of that conflict** |
| **Stephen L. Wasby** (*Political Science — The Discipline and its Dimensions: An Introduction*; 1972) | The absence clause | **Where there is politics, it is said, there is controversy; where there are issues, there is politics. Where no controversy exists, where no issues are being debated, politics does not exist.** |
| **J.D.B. Miller** (*The Nature of Politics*; 1962) | Direction, and the machinery | **Political activity … arises out of disagreement, and it is concerned with the use of government to resolve conflict in the direction of change or in the prevention of change** |

- The chapter's own summary: **the political process postulates the existence of a disagreement or conflict, and efforts for the resolution of that conflict through the authority of government.**
- 🔑 Miller's **or in the prevention of change** is what allows the Marxist account of suppression (§15) to count as politics at all.
- ⚠️ The three stack, they do not compete: conflict and its reconciliation (Ball) → its necessity (Wasby) → direction and machinery (Miller).
- 🔑 And the chapter immediately adds the limit: **every situation involving a conflict and efforts for its resolution cannot be called a 'political situation'.**

## §4 — Ball's example, and the chapter's two corrections *(L4; p.96)*

- The chapter states plainly that **an example of a political situation given by Alan Ball does not appear to be a fit case**. The example: **Two children in a nursery with one toy which they both want at the same time present a political situation**, with the mother arbitrating.
- ❌ The chapter rejects it: **this example suffers from two defects**.
  1. **In the first place, it refers to a 'private' conflict between two children which does not qualify for being a political situation.** ⚠️ The chapter adds the matching case: **similarly, a disagreement between husband and wife on the family budget does not present a political situation** — which is why its **conversion** at §9 matters.
  2. **In the second place, Alan Ball's example contemplates 'resort to violence' as one of the possible methods of conflict-resolution. But, properly speaking, resort to violence or war does not qualify for a political solution.**
- The public test: **only that conflict can be considered the proper subject of politics which arises at a 'public' level, i.e. in which two or more major groups are involved**; a political conflict **demands a 'universal' solution, not a private one**.
- ⚠️ Scale and subject matter are **not** criteria — the chapter widens both: **whether local, regional, national or international, or at the economic, cultural, linguistic, religious or ethnic plane**.
- ⚠️ The single-organisation caution belongs here too: the politics of **church, university or a factory**, **as long as this so-called politics is confined to a single organization with little effect on public life**, **cannot be considered the proper subject of study in political science**. ⚠️ The extraction prints **co-called**, catalogued.
- The qualifying methods: a political solution **emanates from negotiation, persuasion, arbitration, compromise, pressure, counting of votes or any similar tactic short of war**.
- 🔑 **No doubt, some political disputes do culminate in war, but war is a symptom of the failure of a political solution.** War is a failure mode of politics, not an exclusion from it.

## §5 — Easton, and the architecture of authority *(L5; pp.96-97)*

- **David Easton**: politics is the **authoritative allocation of values** for a society — **a short but compact definition** involving **three terms**.

| Term | Chapter's gloss |
|---|---|
| **Values** | **things considered valuable, whether they be spiritual or material** |
| **Allocation** | **distribution of these things to various individuals or groups**, accomplished **through policy which consists of a 'web of decisions'**; **decision** denotes the **selection among alternatives**, and **policy implies arriving at a decision as well as its implementation** |
| **Authoritative** | **a policy is authoritative when the people to whom it is intended to apply or who are affected by it consider that they must or ought to obey it**; and **authority does not signify the use of brute force; it denotes the capacity to secure more-or-less willing compliance from its subjects for a particular decision or course of action** |

- 🔑 Easton in his own words: **Every society provides some mechanisms, however rudimentary they may be, for authoritatively resolving differences about the ends that are to be pursued, that is for deciding who is to get what there is of desirable things. An authoritative allocation of some values is unavoidable.** ⚠️ Mark the last clause: it is what the Marxist end-of-politics thesis (§15) contradicts.
- The chapter's verdict on what Easton adds: it **brings in the element of authoritativeness to the process of conflict-resolution**.
- ⚠️ **Unresolved in the chapter:** a policy counts as authoritative when obeyed **purely from fear of consequences** (§10), yet authority **does not signify the use of brute force**. Any line between the two is **your** reconstruction and must be labelled.

## §6 — Roberts; and politics against economics *(L6; p.97)*

- **Geoffrey K. Roberts** (*A Dictionary of Political Analysis*; 1971) first distinguishes the two senses — the term refers to **both an activity and to the study of that activity** — and then defines the activity: **As an activity, politics is the process in a social system … by which the goals of that system are selected, ordered in terms of priority both temporally and concerning resource allocation, and implemented.**
- ⚠️ The chapter labels what follows: **taking a liberal view of politics**, Roberts holds that the political process **involves both cooperation and the resolution of conflict, by means of the exercise of political authority, and if necessary, coercion**.
- On scope: politics **usually involves the activities of groups of various kinds, including sometimes groups of a specifically political type, such as political parties**.
- The discriminator is **asymmetric**:

| | Economics | Politics |
|---|---|---|
| Sphere | **public or private** | **exclusively** public |
| Purpose | allocation as such | **in pursuance of 'public goals'** |

- 🔑 The pair of terms to carry: **public goals** (here) and **public conflict** (§4). Together they form the publicness test.

## §7 — Politicization *(L7; pp.97-98)*

- **The extent of involvement of individuals and various groups in politics depends upon the level of their 'politicization'.**
- The term applies **either to issues, (e.g. politicization of caste) or to human beings**; in the latter case it **implies the drawing of individuals or groups into political activity, particularly giving them a role in the making of public decisions**.
- Five regime illustrations: an **absolute monarchy** — **where all public decisions are taken by the monarch and his close associates** — exemplifies **the low level of politicization**; **Greek city-states represented the high level of politicization by drawing all 'freemen' into political activity; yet it was by no means universal since it excluded the very large majority of slaves and aliens**; under a **military dictatorship** politicization is **confined to the military junta**; **it is only under a democracy that politicization is intended to be universal**; and when **an emergency is declared**, politicization is **restricted to the ruling groups as long as the declaration of emergency is in operation**.
- 🔑 Two guards: politicization is a **ratio** against a named population, and **intended** is a hedge about the democratic claim.
- ❌ The chapter's whole treatment of caste is the parenthesis **(e.g. politicization of caste)**. ➡️ Caste owners.

## §8 — General characteristic 1: the three conditions *(L8; p.98)*

- **A political situation necessarily involves a conflict or dispute regarding allocation of rare and valuable resources in society.**
- Two exclusions stated before the rule: **if something is valuable but available in abundance, there will be no dispute for its allocation**; and **if something is both valuable and rare but the people are just complacent or ascetic, there is hardly any possibility of conflict over its allocation**.
- 🔑 The rule itself: **conflict occurs when something is valuable, rare and desired by many, thus giving rise to a political situation.** And the trigger: **it is the clash of interests or conflicting claims or expectations of rewards that give rise to politics.**
- The nine examples, explicitly **some of the examples of prevalent 'values'**: **power, enlightenment, wealth, well-being, health, skill, affection, rectitude and deference**.
- The concrete list: conflicting claims for the allocation of **bus service, schools, hospitals, public parks, markets, offices, business and employment opportunities, etc.**
- 🔑 **Deference** is **positional**: it cannot be increased for everyone at once, so some conflicts cannot be dissolved by producing more. ⚠️ Mark this against §15's technology route.

## §9 — General characteristic 2: the public-goals filter *(L9; pp.98-99)*

- **All conflicts for resource-allocation do not give rise to a political situation. Politics is exclusively concerned with conflicts involving public goals and public issues requiring public decisions for their resolution.**
- Five paired conflicts that may or may not qualify: **between worker and employer, between customer and shopkeeper, between tenant and landlord, between the passengers on the same bus or between the users of the same road or of different roads meeting at a crossroad** — each **becomes a public issue when it is concerned with broad categories of people and calls for a solution at the public level**.
- The conversion case: **a conflict between husband and wife on some petty domestic affair does not present a political situation. But if it takes the form of a question of respective claims of men and women in the context of their mutual relationships demanding solution at the public level, it becomes a political question and a fit subject of politics.**
- 🔑 The **facts** do not change; the **description** does. ❌ No substantive gender claim is made. ➡️ Topic 06.
- **Michael Curtis** (*Comparative Government and Politics*; 1978): **The student of politics is normally concerned with inquiry into matters of public concern, with the behaviour and acts that may concern a society as a totality or which may ultimately be resolved by the exercise of legitimate coercion.** ⚠️ The connective is **or**.

## §10 — General characteristic 3: authoritative decisions *(L10; p.99)*

- **The resolution of a political conflict is meaningful only when it is treated as binding by those who are affected by the decision embodying the resolution.**
- **According to David Easton, a policy may be accepted as authoritative on several grounds: 'moral, traditional or customary, or purely from fear of consequences' (*The Political System*; 1953).** In other words, **authority involves 'legitimacy' or 'power' or both**.
- Two worked allocations, running in opposite directions:

| | What the chapter describes | Legitimating belief supplied? |
|---|---|---|
| A | Seats in a public bus reserved **for 'ladies only'**, and **certain posts in bureaucracy or seats in legislatures … reserved for the scheduled castes and scheduled tribes**; and **the rich pay higher taxes, particularly income tax and wealth tax, for maintaining government hospitals, schools and public transport at cheaper rates, which are largely availed of by the poor** | ✅ Yes — treated as **'legitimate' due to the prevailing belief that the weaker sections are entitled to special protection** |
| B | **The poor are made to pay large sums in the form of say, sales tax, on each commodity of their use, sharing the burden for the maintenance of police, defence, etc. which are chiefly meant for the protection of the property of the rich** | ⚠️ **No belief is supplied** |

- Both are bound by the same reason: **the rich cannot refuse to pay this tax on the plea that it is not utilized for their benefit, since it involves authoritative allocation**, and **the poor, too, cannot refuse to pay this tax since it involves authoritative allocation**.
- The machinery: **then there is a machinery to implement the authoritative decisions which symbolizes application of force or threat of physical force or coercion for the enforcement of a particular 'value-allocation'**, and **an authority is always backed by law, executive orders, custom and a strong public opinion in its favour on the one hand, and by police, magistrates and prisons on the other**.
- 🔑 The revisability guard: **however, no authoritative allocation can be treated as eternal or unalterable. It holds the field until a new arrangement is established as authoritative.**
- ❌ **2009** illustrations; no statute, article or rate is given. ➡️ Polity.

## §11 — Characteristics 4 and 5, and the fork *(L11; pp.99-100)*

- **4 — interest groups:** **a political situation arises from a clash of interests of different large groups of society**, and those groups **must be conscious of their particular interests, and be more or less organized for pursuing those interests**. 🔑 The direction is bottom-up: **the conflicting claims and demands for the 'authoritative allocation of values' emanate from these 'interest groups', which require policy decisions**, and **political parties appear on the scene with a view to formulating large policy proposals incorporating the interests of their support-groups according to their ideologies**.
- **5 — conflict-resolution:** **the end-product of the political process is supposed to be the resolution of the conflict from which the process had started.** ⚠️ **Supposed to be** is the hedge that keeps the Marxist view in play.
- The fork is stated here and deferred: **the liberal view of politics holds that politics is an instrument of reconciliation of the conflicting interests in society**, whereas the **Marxist view of politics insists that the interests of the two major social classes — the 'haves' and 'have-nots' (the property-holders and the propertyless) — are irreconcilable**.

## §12 — Politics as a dimension of the social process *(L12; p.100)*

- The build, in four steps: the political process is **not an independent or closed process, but takes place within the larger arena of the social process**; **by 'process' we mean sets of interactions among the components of a system**, so **where there is a process, there is a system**; **the political process constitutes a political system**; and **the political system itself is a sub-system of the larger social system. That is why the political process has been described as a dimension of social process.**
- Two derived fields, named once and dropped: **there are interactions between politics and other components of the social system, such as economy and culture. The products of these interactions are 'political economy' and 'political culture' respectively.** ❌ No definition, components or typology for either. ⚠️ The extraction prints a doubled comma at **social system,,**, catalogued.
- ❌ No input-output model, no feedback loop, no equilibrium analysis. ➡️ The approaches owner.
- ⚠️ Discipline point: name what the chapter names, and route the development elsewhere.

## §13 — The three views: what is actually being disagreed about *(L13; pp.100-101)*

- The chapter's framing: **liberal**, **Marxist** and **communitarian** views.
- 🔑 The disagreement is **not** about how to settle conflicts; it is about **what society is**. Everything else follows from the **Structure of Society** row (§19).
- The communitarian motivation: communitarianism **arose from the criticism of liberalism as a means to human happiness** — the modern man **enjoys better conditions of living; but his economic security has failed to make him happy for want of emotional security**; **he feels lonely in a huge crowd around him**; **he lives in society without the sense of belonging to it**. ❌ Offered as the doctrine's motivating claim, with no evidence.
- What it proposes: it **seeks to restore the broken bond between individual and society**, making the individual realise **he owes his existence and personality to society**, since **different individuals are not isolated units but they constitute the threads of the social fabric** and **can serve their interest by serving the common interest, not by seeking their self-interest individually**.
- The emphasis clause: **While liberalism insists on the rights of the individual, communitarianism focuses on his duties and obligations.** ⚠️ **Focuses on** ≠ rejects rights.
- **Early indications of this view may be traced to Aristotle (ancient Greek philosopher), J.J. Rousseau (1712-78), G.W.F. Hegel (1770-1831) and T.H. Green (1836-82).** 🔶 The owner requires these be treated as **precursors/anticipations**, not communitarians.
- **Its contemporary exponents include Alasdair MacIntyre (1929- ), Charles Taylor (1931- ), and Michael Sandel (1953- ) etc.** ⚠️ MacIntyre's standard form is now **(1929-2025)**; the extraction prints **Maclntyre**, catalogued.

## §14 — The liberal view *(L14-L15; pp.101-102)*

- The premise is **the pluralistic view of society**: **different individuals seek their interests as members of different groups**.
- Two labels, and the chapter gives both: politics is **a group activity as well as a state activity** — a group activity because interests are pursued through **organized groups**, a state activity because **the authoritative allocation against the demands and claims of various groups is made by the state**.
- The circuit: **demands and supports (such as obedience to laws and regulations, payment of taxes, etc.) emanate from various interest-groups while policies and decisions are delivered by the state.**
- 🔑 The always-claim, stated twice in one sentence: **whenever there is an apparent clash of interests between several groups, some way can always be found to ensure the reconciliation of their conflicting interests. For instance, you can always form a rule or evolve a policy which will satisfy the parties to a dispute …**
- The conclusion, with its coercion tail: **Any conflict, disagreement or dispute arising in society is capable of resolution by conciliation and legitimate coercion. Thus, politics is an instrument of securing order and justice in society.**
- The common-good mechanism: **awareness of the conflicting interests amongst various groups and their interaction leads to an awareness of the 'common interest'**, so that **at one point, the contending groups are prepared to accept the solution which is conducive to the 'common good'**; and the **'common good', according to liberal theory, accommodates the interests of all parties involved in the conflict**. ⚠️ The p.107 table compresses this into **Stage of Equilibrium between Conflicting Interests** — the equilibrium is reached **through** conflict, not instead of it.
- Four showpiece settlements, each a positive-sum claim: a policy ensuring **better conditions for workers and higher profits for employers**; **'a reasonable price' which will satisfy both the producer and the consumer, the supplier and the customer**; a **'queue system' which will facilitate the distribution of rationed commodities**; the **'red and green signal system'** providing **order and safety to the users of cross-roads**.
- Where it leads: **this concern for the 'common good' has led us to the concept of the modern 'welfare state'.** ❌ The chapter derives the welfare state in that one sentence and develops it no further here.
- ⚠️ Testing the always-claim: the queue and the traffic signal are **coordination** cases with no opposed interest; they do not test it. One genuine counter-example refutes a universal claim.
- 📰 **2009** text; no statute, scheme or country is named anywhere in this section. ➡️ Polity.

## §15 — The Marxist view *(L16-L17; pp.102-104)*

- Base claim: **political institutions and activities are an outgrowth of the prevailing economic system, especially the mode of production**; **all social relations, including political relations, are shaped by the prevailing economic relations in society**; and so **conflicting economic interests are, therefore, the motive force behind all politics**.
- Against liberal multiplicity: where liberal theory **envisages innumerable political situations and clash of interests on multifarious issues** the Marxist theory **regards the clash of economic interests as the fundamental issue of social conflict**, holding that **other issues are superficial and their resolution, if any, cannot end the conflict**.
- The two-way conditional: **if the economic issue is solved, all other issues will automatically disappear. Conversely, if the economic issue is evaded, settlement of all other issues will be a futile exercise.**
- The rational system of production has **two** conditions: **(a) highest advancement of technology so as to get maximum production; and (b) social ownership of the means of production and distribution so that all production caters to the needs of the masses, not of the chosen few**; and **only a rational system of production is conducive to cooperative effort**. 🔑 The first attacks **rarity**; the second attacks **cornering**.
- The cornering claim: **a small minority manages to corner ownership of the major means of production and forces the rest of the population to live on hard labour**, dividing society into **the 'haves' and the 'have-nots', the masters and the servants, the exploiters and the exploited**, whose **interests are irreconcilable**.
- **Engels** (*The Origin of the Family, Private Property and the State*; 1884): **it is, as a rule, the state of the most powerful, economically dominant class, which through the medium of the state, becomes also the politically dominant class, and thus acquires new means of holding down and exploiting the oppressed class.** ⚠️ Keep the hedge **as a rule**.
- ***Communist Manifesto*** (1848): class struggle is **a fight that each time ended either in a revolutionary reconstitution of society at large, or in the common ruin of the contending classes.** ⚠️ The second disjunct is usually dropped and is doing real work.
- Politics as suppression: **politics is used by the dominant class to suppress the conflict. This gives the impression of voluntary compromise, but in reality it is a compromise imposed by the dominant class on the dependent class. It even creates an 'illusion of consent' by administering strong ideological doses.**
- ⚠️ **The illusion of consent is unfalsifiable as stated.** The chapter gives no criterion for distinguishing genuine from illusory consent, so no observed agreement can count against the thesis.
- Reverse gear: **this process will continue even after the 'socialist revolution', but in 'reverse gear'** — **socialism (popularly dubbed communism) is characterized by the suppression of the dispossessed capitalists by the workers (proletariat) in order to forestall a counterrevolution and to pave the way for the blossoming of communism**, a **classless society**.
- 🔑 **With the elimination of the classes, class conflict will disappear and state and politics would no longer be required; the state will 'wither away'.** And: **the end of class-conflict marks an end of politics itself.** ⚠️ Both the state **and** politics go. This collides with Easton's **unavoidable**.
- ❌ No historical materialism, base/superstructure vocabulary or stages of history. ➡️ Topic 04.

## §16 — The communitarian view *(L18-L20; pp.104-105)*

- The nature claim: **the essence of human nature lies in the spirit of cooperation, not of conflict. Hence mutual aid or cooperation is the foundation of political organization.**
- The diagnosis, stated earlier at p.100: **the impression of conflict arises from misunderstanding of our real interest: the common good which comprehends everybody's real interest** — and so the view **redefines politics as the pursuit of the common good rather than as an attempt at resolution of some pre-existing conflict**.
- **Aristotle**'s analogy: **the relation between individual and the state was similar to that between an organ and the organism**; **the state was an indispensable instrument of good life for all human beings**; hence **different individuals must cooperate with each other in order to secure good life for everyone**. 🔑 **Instrument**, and the distribution of the end across **all** and **everyone**, block the collectivist reading.
- Identity: **an individual develops his identity, talents and pursuits in life only from his place in the community.**
- **Community** as the chapter defines it: **a set of social relationships based on sharing common characteristics, common values and common interests.**
- 🔑 The define/discover pair, in the chapter's own sentence: **whereas liberals encourage each person to define and seek his own 'good' within the political structure, communitarians direct him to discover and pursue his 'good' as an integral part of the 'good of the community'** — and **this can be accomplished only through cooperation, not through competition**.

| | Liberal | Communitarian |
|---|---|---|
| Relation to the good | **define and seek his own 'good'** | **discover and pursue his 'good' as an integral part of the 'good of the community'** |
| Is error possible about your own good? | no | **yes** — hence the misunderstanding diagnosis |
| The common good is (p.107 table) | **Stage of Equilibrium between Conflicting Interests** | **Uniform Good that Comprehends the Interest of Each Member of Society** |

- The indebtedness argument: **the individual owes his existence and his potentialities to society; hence he has no right to apply these potentialities in the so-called self-interest, disregarding the interest of society. His commitment to society is an essential feature of his personality.** ⚠️ Note the earlier p.101 form as well — **he owes his existence and personality to society**.
- The concession: **in order to flourish in life an individual requires a place in a well-functioning community** — the chapter's own admission that communities can function badly.
- The three contemporary thinkers, as the chapter presents them:

| Thinker | Work and date as the chapter gives it | The chapter's claim |
|---|---|---|
| **Alasdair MacIntyre** | ***After Virtue***; 1981 | individuals **develop and perfect virtue through cooperative human activity**; if the state allows **'socially disconnected'** individuals to pursue **so-called 'self-appointed goals'**, the result is **social disintegration and moral disaster**, already evident in **the prevalence of crime and violence, the breakdown of the family, and the widespread drug abuse** in **some modern liberal states** |
| **Charles Taylor** | ***Philosophical Papers***; 1985 | attacks the liberals' **'atomistic' conception of human beings which ignores the fact that a human being must be 'situated' in a society in order to develop his personality** |
| **Michael Sandel** | ***Liberalism and the Limits of Justice***; 1982 | criticizes the liberals' conception of the **disjointed nature of the people**; views human nature as **'embedded' in a particular time, place and culture**; insists on creating a **'deeper commonality'** informed by **'shared self-understanding' as well as affection** |

- The chapter's summary: **in a nutshell, the communitarian view treats politics as an activity concerned with the identification and pursuit of their common interest.** ⚠️ **Identification** comes first — exactly what the discover-model predicts.
- ❌ MacIntyre's symptom list carries **no study, country, period or data** — a **2009** assertion, and its causal attribution is unargued. Cite it as his **claim**, never as evidence.
- ❌ **Rawls is not named anywhere in the chapter.** Everything about the original position belongs to §18.

## §17 — Miliband, and the chapter's own verdict *(L21; pp.105-106)*

- The Conclusion is framed around **the nature and source of the conflict from which politics emanates, the prospects of conflict-resolution, and the utility of politics itself**.
- **Ralph Miliband** (*Marxism and Politics*; 1977): **In the liberal view of politics, conflict exists in terms of 'problems' which need to be 'solved'. The hidden assumption is that conflict does not, or need not, run very deep; that it can be 'managed' by the exercise of reason and good will and a readiness to compromise and agree … The Marxist approach to conflict is very different. It is not a matter of 'problems' to be 'solved' but of a state of domination and subjection to be ended by a total transformation of the conditions which give rise to it.**
- 🔑 The chapter's own objection: the liberal approach **seems to be based on 'reason' and devoted to 'justice'. But the difficulty with this approach is that it fails to distinguish between 'compromise' and 'cooperation'. 'Compromise' can be effected without creating the conditions of 'freedom'. Real 'cooperation' among members of different groups or classes can be generated only after elimination of the conditions of 'dominance' and 'dependence'. Liberal writers use 'compromise' and 'cooperation' synonymously which obscures the whole issue.**
- The functional/dysfunctional pair: Miliband further elaborates that the conflict **is not only civilized, but also civilizing**, and the chapter adds that conflict is **'functional', a stabilizing rather than a 'disrupting force'**, whereas **Marxist theory regards conflict as 'dysfunctional'. It is symptomatic of a rift within society which tends to perpetuate the practice of exploitation until its character is transformed by a 'socialist' revolution.** ⚠️ The extraction prints **is*'functional'**, catalogued.
- The Marxist verdict on liberal settlements, with its conditional: **The antagonisms are irreconcilable, and the notion of genuine harmony is a deception or a delusion, at least in relation to class societies.**
- ⚠️ The compromise/cooperation distinction is the **chapter's**, introduced by **the difficulty with this approach is**, not Miliband's.

## §18 — 🔶 Owner extensions *(L23)*

> 🔶 **Never cite any of this to Gauba.** It is the canonical owner's extension material and is examinable.

- 🔶 **Three differentiated mechanisms** — the single highest-yield extension:

| Thinker | Mechanism | Its limit |
|---|---|---|
| 🔶 MacIntyre | the self is constituted through **practices**, **internal goods** and **tradition** | **risks relativism**; his answer — comparing traditions by their **capacity to resolve their own internal crises** — **remains contested** |
| 🔶 Taylor | the **dialogical self**; identity formed through **recognition**; misrecognition can **inflict harm** | requires a standard for which recognition claims are legitimate; the owner records this as open |
| 🔶 Sandel | the **unencumbered self** behind Rawls's **veil of ignorance** is not a possible person; justice cannot be prior to the good | the liberal reply (below) |

- 🔶 **Objection chain 1 — neutrality:** the liberal state claims neutrality between conceptions of the good → the communitarian replies that liberal neutrality is **impossible and self-defeating**, since liberal politics already presupposes and promotes the autonomous rights-bearing chooser → **liberal reply, following Rawls:** neutrality is **a political device for fair terms of cooperation among people who reasonably disagree about the good life, not a metaphysical claim that no view of the self is presupposed**, and **procedural fairness across competing comprehensive doctrines is a defensible, minimal shared commitment, not an empty pretence** → **residual:** the owner requires this be presented as **an ongoing, unresolved debate rather than declaring a winner**, and the chapter's own **well-functioning** concession admits that communities can fail, so neither side has discharged its burden. ❌ Do not restate the liberal reply as an appeal to the dangers of state perfectionism; that is a different argument and not the owner's.
- 🔶 **Objection chain 2 — the unencumbered self:** (i) the original position strips constitutive attachments; (ii) so it cannot model actual moral agents; (iii) so principles chosen there cannot bind them → **liberal reply:** the veil is **a heuristic device for modelling impartiality, not a claim that actual persons are or should be unencumbered** → **counter-reply:** the device still privileges the capacity to revise one's ends → **residual:** both sides accept that some abstraction is necessary; they differ on how much.
- 🔶 **Indian application, bounded:** the **structure** of Fundamental Rights, Directive Principles and **Fundamental Duties** illustrates a constitutional order carrying both individual-rights and community-regarding commitments — ❌ **without asserting specific article numbers** ➡️ Polity. Reservation debates are **communitarian-adjacent**, not communitarian. Gandhi may be used **as an analogy** only ➡️ Topic 05. Fraternity detached from liberty and equality risks majoritarianism.

## §19 — The summary table and the three boxes *(L22; pp.106-107)*

- The eight rows of **Different Views of Politics** are reproduced in full in **Map 1**. Learn them by **deriving** them from the first row, not by rote.
- The scope conditions (p.106, **not** in the table):

| View | Suited to a society that is |
|---|---|
| Liberal | **not afflicted by wide disparities of wealth, prestige and power** |
| Marxist | **sharply divided into dominant and dependent classes, where dependent class is not left with any alternative for peaceful solution** |
| Communitarian | whose members are **largely satisfied with their status** and **prepared to cooperate to improve their lot** |

- The Marxist qualifier: conditions of **dominance** and **dependence** exist **at many subtle levels**, so the second row is wider than it looks.
- 🔑 The futility clause — the only explicit failure condition in the chapter: **if a society is characterized by wide disparities of power and differences of opinion, it would be futile to look into its problems from the communitarian point of view.**
- 📰 **The former USSR (1917)** and **the People's Republic of China (1949)** are **book-period** illustrations supporting only the finding that a classless society is **extremely difficult to realize**.
- The three definition boxes:

| Box | Chapter's definition | Note |
|---|---|---|
| **Liberty** | **a condition in which an individual does not face unreasonable restraints while making full use of his abilities in fulfilling his self-appointed goals** | 🔑 **Self-appointed goals** is precisely MacIntyre's target |
| **Equality** | **in the Marxist context** — **a condition in which individuals are not divided into dominant and dependent classes due to private ownership of the major means of production** | ⚠️ Only this box is context-flagged |
| **Fraternity** | **a sentiment which informs the members of a community who share a common culture, common values, an awareness of the common interest, and engage in the common effort to achieve a social goal** | ⚠️ Defined as **a sentiment**, not a right or a structure |

- ❌ The **Exponents** row names **Bentham**, **J.S. Mill**, **Laski**, **Robert Dahl**, **Lenin**, **Gramsci** and **Mao** — **seven** names for whom the chapter supplies no date, argument or discussion. ⚠️ **Laski** alone is named elsewhere, at p.94, only as the author of **A Grammar of Politics and An Introduction to Politics**. Attribute nothing to them on this authority.
- The overall verdict: **none of these views of politics may be treated as universally acceptable.**

## §20 — Answer architecture *(L24)*

- 🔑 **Ontology before institutions.** Identify the image of society first; the view of politics, the common good and the remedy all follow from it.
- The three-tier provenance rule, which this session applies throughout:

| Tier | Marker | Citation rule |
|---|---|---|
| The chapter | plain | cite by PDF page (printed page = PDF - 14) |
| Owner extension | 🔶 | **never** cite to Gauba |
| Outside both | — | not used this session, except one verified life-date |

- Structures that work:
  - **10 marks:** definition → one mechanism → one qualification.
  - **15 marks:** two views contrasted on the **Structure of Society** row → the mechanism each generates → one conceded residual.
  - **20 marks:** all three views → the scope conditions → the chapter's own objections (compromise ≠ cooperation; the futility clause) → a conditional verdict.
- ⚠️ The conditional verdict is the signature move of this chapter. Never declare a winner: **none of these views … may be treated as universally acceptable**, and each is matched to a type of society.

**Coverage confirmations**

- ✅ All **24** lessons carry a pre-teach checklist, a progress line, at least one visual, and exactly one concept check with one model answer and one misconception.
- ✅ Core owner sections **§§1-12** are covered: the conceptual map and significance (§§1-2) at Lessons 1, 7 and 13 and in the master visual; definitions (§3) across Lessons 3, 5-8, 10, 11 and 19; the chapter spine (§4) at Lessons 1, 2, 5 and 12; distinctions (§5) at Lessons 4, 9, 10, 19 and 21; thinkers (§6) at Lessons 13-20; examples (§7) at Lessons 8, 10 and 15; the comparison material (§8) at Lessons 11, 14, 18 and 22 and in Maps 1 and 4; traps (§9) at Lessons 4 and 9 and throughout Remediation; cross-links and boundaries (§10) in the Subject boundary below; the revision capsule (§11) at Lesson 22 and in these register notes; and sources (§12) in this Source Ledger.
- ✅ Core owner **extension** sections **§§13-17** are carried without double-claiming: **§13** differentiated mechanisms, **§14** precursors, **§15** the two objection-reply chains and **§16** the cautious Indian application at **Lesson 23** and register §18, all marked 🔶 and cited to **no** page of the chapter; and **§17** the executable answer architecture at **Lesson 24**.
- ✅ Advanced owner sections **§§1-12** are covered, including **§7** internal debates (Lessons 21, 22 and 🔶 23), **§8** the comparative matrix (Map 1), **§§9-10** application and prompts (Lesson 24 and the PYQ approaches), **§11** exclusions (Subject boundary) and **§12** factual-risk checks (Lessons 10, 20, 22 and the open uncertainties).
- ✅ The **Book evidence** column reproduces each lesson's own pre-teach page range rather than a synthetic contiguous partition, so adjacent rows overlap where a section straddles a page and the ranges are **not** a tiling of pp.94-107. ⚠️ Lessons 14 and 15 share pp.101-102 because the chapter's two liberal subsections run together across that page break; Lesson 23 carries **no** anchor; Lesson 24 is a synthesis over the whole range.
- ✅ Practice: **6** cumulative concept checks, **24** per-lesson concept checks, **6** original Mains models with unique scoring notes, **80** remediation entries, **9** verified Philosophy Optional anchors with approaches, and **5** master maps.
- ⚠️ **Not covered, by rule**: historical materialism, base and superstructure, surplus value and the stages of history (➡️ Topic 04); Aristotle's *polis*, *telos* and virtue theory (➡️ the Aristotle owners); Rawls's theory of justice, the difference principle and *A Theory of Justice* as a text (➡️ the justice owner); Weber's typology of authority and the faces-of-power debate (➡️ the power and authority owner); systems analysis as a method, with inputs, outputs and feedback (➡️ the approaches owner); multiculturalism as a doctrine and the recognition literature (➡️ Humanism, Secularism and Multiculturalism); rights-and-obligation theory (➡️ Individual and State); liberal democracy as a form of government (➡️ Forms of Government); caste as a substantive subject (➡️ the caste owners); constitutional articles, statutes, schemes and case law (➡️ Polity); and any post-2009 substantive fact, figure, report or policy.

## Page-anchor convention

- The local PDF's page numbering runs **ahead** of the printed page numbering, and the offset is **not constant across the book**.
- **This topic's chapter**: PDF pp.**94-107** = printed pp.**80-93** — offset **14**.
- Every citation in this file gives the **PDF** page, with the printed page in parentheses where both are stated. ⚠️ No universal offset should be inferred; other chapters of the same book used by sibling topics in this subject run at a different offset.
- ❌ No page outside **PDF pp.94-107** is cited anywhere in this file as evidence for this topic's substance.

## Evidence typing in this file

| Marker | Meaning |
|---|---|
| Blockquote | Source wording reproduced as such, subject to the OCR and presentational normalisations catalogued below |
| ✅ | A claim the chapter states directly, with its wording carried as closely as the extraction allows |
| **Bold** | Ordinary emphasis, key terms, and editorial labels. ⚠️ Bold is **not** a quotation marker and carries no claim of verbatim identity |
| ⚠️ | Inference, caution, unresolved tension, or a limit on what the evidence supports |
| ❌ | An explicit boundary — material this topic does not own, or a claim that must not be made |
| ➡️ | A routing pointer to another topic's or subject's owner |
| 🔑 | A high-yield point worth memorising in the chapter's own terms |
| 🔶 | Content from the subject owner's extension sections (basic §§13-16 and advanced §§7, 12) carrying **no** anchor in PDF pp.94-107 |
| 📰 | A dated anchor, carried with its date attached |

⚠️ Blockquotes and spans explicitly introduced as the chapter's wording are reproduced from the extraction, subject to the corrections catalogued immediately below. Phrases carried inside ordinary prose are reproduced as closely as the extraction allows and are identified by context; where compression was necessary the sentence is written as this session's own prose rather than presented as the chapter's. **No exhaustive identity is claimed for every bolded token, and bold alone is never a quotation claim.**
